View five news elements
Time
Published 2026-08-03; event described as occurring in early August 2026 with a time range spanning mid-June to early August 2026.
Location and population
South Australia, Victoria, and nationally across Australia; affected populations include silver gulls (seagulls), greater crested terns, migratory seabirds (giant petrels, brown skua), and potentially domestic poultry (chickens).
Event
Detection of H5N1 avian influenza in silver gulls in South Australia, marking a new stage in local spread beyond previously affected migratory seabirds and terns; reported by ABC News and Australian Chief Veterinary Officer Beth Cookson; event is confirmed and ongoing.
Scale, impact and risk
49 greater crested terns found dead in South Australia; potential for widespread spread due to silver gull mobility and congregation with other bird species; risk of onward spread to domestic poultry (e.g., chickens) and freshwater birds (e.g., ducks); H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b previously caused mass die-offs globally including gulls and seals.
Response, status and uncertainty
Australian and South Australian authorities monitoring and investigating; surveillance ongoing; discussions about mandatory housing orders for free-range poultry; no human cases reported in supplied evidence; virus considered here to stay; unresolved questions about future spread to waterfowl and inland wetlands.

“仅仅是开始”:H5N1 禽流感首次大规模死亡得到证实 - SMH.com.au

‘Just the beginning’: First mass deaths from H5N1 bird flu confirmed - SMH.com.au
新闻简报Australia has confirmed local transmission of H5N1 avian influenza in wildlife, with mass deaths already reported among greater crested terns and an estimated 13,000 seal pups on sub-Antarctic Heard Island. Federal ministers warn the outbreak is ‘just the beginning,’ signaling extensive spread across mainland bird and mammal populations. Conservation groups liken the unfolding crisis to earlier devastation in South America, where millions of birds and marine mammals perished within months. The government has pledged AUD 113 million for rapid-response measures, including farm quarantines and feral animal control, while urging the public not to feed wild birds or approach sick animals. Scientists caution that heavy inland rains could accelerate spread via waterfowl, with dire consequences for species such as Tasmanian devils and platypuses.
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 8 月 3 日 17:37(澳大利亚东部标准时间);事件被描述为最近几天持续升级(N1、N4、N9)。
地点与对象
南澳大利亚州南部沿海地区(罗布岩和博丹岩)和亚南极赫德岛领土;更广泛的澳大利亚大陆面临风险;野生动物种群,包括大凤头燕鸥、银鸥、海豹、海狮、负鼠、塔斯马尼亚袋獾、袋鼬、鸭嘴兽和水禽(N5、N6、N29、N37、N39)。
事件
澳大利亚当局确认当地野生动物中存在H5N1 传播 传播,标志着病例显着升级;被描述为大规模野生动物死亡的“只是开始”;联邦部长和保护领导人警告称,可能会造成广泛传播和重大损失;建议公众不要接近或触摸病禽(N4、N8、N9、N26、N31)。
规模、影响与风险
Dozens of greater crested terns tested positive; an estimated 13,000 seal pups killed on Heard Island (over 75% of new births); global context of over 300 million animal deaths attributed to H5N1; risk to human health remains low; potential for rapid inland spread via waterfowl and scavenging mammals; severe ecological and agricultural impacts anticipated (N4, N29, N36, N10).
应对、状态与不确定性
Federal government announced AUD 113 million rapid-response package including farm quarantines, transport restrictions, and feral animal control; authorities surveying seals and sea lions; public urged to avoid feeding wild birds and discourage bird aggregations; scientists warn of possible mammal infections and extinctions; ongoing investigation into transmission routes and species impacts (N8, N11, N33, N37, N40).

‘Just the beginning’: First mass deaths from H5N1 bird flu confirmed - SMH.com.au

News Brief
Australia has confirmed local transmission of H5N1 avian influenza in wildlife, with mass deaths already reported among greater crested terns and an estimated 13,000 seal pups on sub-Antarctic Heard Island. Federal ministers warn the outbreak is ‘just the beginning,’ signaling extensive spread across mainland bird and mammal populations. Conservation groups liken the unfolding crisis to earlier devastation in South America, where millions of birds and marine mammals perished within months. The government has pledged AUD 113 million for rapid-response measures, including farm quarantines and feral animal control, while urging the public not to feed wild birds or approach sick animals. Scientists caution that heavy inland rains could accelerate spread via waterfowl, with dire consequences for species such as Tasmanian devils and platypuses.

Share AAA 22View all comments Add us as a preferred source on Google Listen to this article 6 min Advertisement Australians should expect to witness mass wildlife deaths after bird flu was found to have become established on the mainland, as the federal government confirmed dozens of greater crested terns had tested positive for the deadly H5N1 variant that has killed more than 300 million animals around the world. A seagull and the crested terns were found in Robe and at Baudin Rocks respectively, off the southern South Australia coast. An Australian silver gull has tested positive for bird flu. Ken Irwin As the poultry industry braces for stock infections, rangers across the country survey water mammals such as seals and endangered sea lions - known to be susceptible - for the signs, while in the nation’s cities seagulls, possums and other scavenging animals could spread of the virus, exemplifying the breadth of threat the virus poses. “Sadly, we do know that once the H5 bird flu is spreading in wildlife, and the natural environment, it is not possible to avoid significant losses – which is what we are starting to see now,” Agriculture Minister Julie Collins said at a press conference on Monday. “Australians should expect to see more spread and larger numbers now of wildlife being affected.” Environment Minister Murray Watt said the mass deaths in South Australia were “just the beginning” in what is likely to be a “long and difficult journey”. While the risk to human health remains low, an outbreak of a different strain in 2024 led to about 2 million chickens being killed, causing egg shortages. Collins said Australia had improved biosecurity preparations to protect the industry, having learned from the impact of the disease around the world. Advertisement From our partners Advertisement Kate Millar, chief executive of Australia’s largest bird conservation group, Bird Life Australia, likened the mass deaths over the weekend to the moment bird flu first hit South America in 2022. Within months the disease had spread 6000 kilometres along the continent’s coasts, killing millions of birds, as well as seals, otters, dolphins and porpoises. Invasive Species Council chief executive Jack Gough warned the impending disaster would be confronting for the public, likening the onset of bird flu to the animal deaths from the South Australian algal bloom and the Black Summer bushfires. Birdlife Australia’s Dr Michael MacDonald said it was not yet clear what impact bird flu on some beloved species, such as the brush turkey, a macropod with limited range outside this country.Steven Siewert “It’s not going to be theoretical. People are going to confront this. There has been complacency around environmental issues, particularly in our federal politics over the last few years, outside of the climate wars,” Gough said. “But as people start to deal with the grief and reality of mass wildlife deaths, the politicians that get out ahead of it with a good story to tell about funding resilience are going to weather that public shift in sentiment a lot better.” Birdlife Australia head of science Michael MacDonald worked with the Royal Society for the Protection of Birds in the UK when bird flu hit there, and recalled a sense of loss and helplessness when witnessing colonies of seabirds collapse in Wales. This modal can be closed by pressing the Escape key or activating the close button. 1:56 Authorities confirm local transmission of H5 bird flu Local transmission of bird flu has been confirmed in South Australia, marking a significant escalation in the outbreak. “Some things happening to nature are our fault, this is not. All we can do is try to protect the animals from other pressures,” he said. An estimated 13,000 seal pups were killed by the virus on Australia’s sub-Antarctic Heard Island territory, more than three-quarters of new births on the island, before it was carried to the mainland, likely by a large pelagic bird such as a petrel. The disease is now likely to be spread by scavenging birds and mammals, such as common silver gulls – what many would recognise as seagulls – as well as cats and foxes. People have been warned not to approach or touch birds that show signs of bird flu. Symptoms include being unable to stand, walk or fly properly, breathing difficulties, and mass deaths in one area. Bird Life Australia said the onset of the virus strengthened the advice against feeding wild birds such as seagulls and magpies, and chief vet Beth Cookson also said authorities would call on Australians not to “encourage situations where there might be greater aggregations of wild birds. That’s certainly one context where the disease could spread further”. Professor John Woinarski, one of Australia’s leading Australian biologists, said heavy monsoons that have seen an inland sea formed in parts of central Australia could also see the disease spread rapidly inland. “There’s bound to be silver gulls and pelicans and ibis and waterfowl flying into inland Australia and the Murray River system and spreading the infection very rapidly and very extensively, and with dire consequences,” he said. He also warned the disease was likely to hit mammals in Australia, such as Tasmanian devils, quolls and perhaps even platypuses. He said the disease had not yet caused known extinctions around the world, but had driven many animals close to the brink. In cities, species such as possums, which are known to scavenge on dead animals, were likely to be hit alongside all birds. The Albanese government has committed $113 million to prepare a rapid response, which may involve quarantining farms and restricting transport between locations, as well as ramping up efforts to reduce the numbers of feral cats, foxes and pigs. Gough said this was critical not just because these animals preyed upon birds and eggs, but because they can carry the disease and damage critical habitats. “We’re about to have ... big breeding events [in Spring], and we want the next two seasons of breeding events to be as good as we can possibly make them, because we’re going to have these waves of bird flu hitting,” Gough said. “It’s basically a numbers game for wildlife now.” Get to the heart of what’s happening with climate change and the environment. Published 3 August 2026 at 17:37 AEST; event described as ongoing with escalation in recent days (N1, N4, N9).
Location and population
Southern South Australia coastal sites (Robe and Baudin Rocks) and sub-Antarctic Heard Island territory; broader Australian mainland at risk; wildlife populations including greater crested terns, silver gulls, seals, sea lions, possums, Tasmanian devils, quolls, platypuses, and waterfowl (N5, N6, N29, N37, N39).
Event
Australian authorities confirmed local transmission of H5N1 avian influenza in wildlife, marking a significant escalation; described as 'just the beginning' of mass wildlife deaths; federal ministers and conservation leaders warn of extensive spread and significant losses; public advised not to approach or touch sick birds (N4, N8, N9, N26, N31).
Scale, impact and risk
Dozens of greater crested terns tested positive; an estimated 13,000 seal pups killed on Heard Island (over 75% of new births); global context of over 300 million animal deaths attributed to H5N1; risk to human health remains low; potential for rapid inland spread via waterfowl and scavenging mammals; severe ecological and agricultural impacts anticipated (N4, N29, N36, N10).
Response, status and uncertainty
Federal government announced AUD 113 million rapid-response package including farm quarantines, transport restrictions, and feral animal control; authorities surveying seals and sea lions; public urged to avoid feeding wild birds and discourage bird aggregations; scientists warn of possible mammal infections and extinctions; ongoing investigation into transmission routes and species impacts (N8, N11, N33, N37, N40).

澳大利亚确认首次大规模海鸟死亡与 H5N1 禽流感有关 - StratNews Global

Australia Confirms First Mass Seabird Deaths Linked To H5N1 Bird Flu - StratNews Global
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 3 日,澳大利亚在阿德莱德以南海岸的 49 只死亡大凤头燕鸥和 35 只患病大凤头燕鸥中检测到病毒后,确认发生了首例与 H5N1 禽流感 有关的大规模海鸟死亡事件。全国多个州共确诊 74 例 H5N1 病例,但家禽养殖场并未受到影响。农业部长朱莉·柯林斯警告说,野生动物可能会更广泛地传播,预计会造成重大损失。澳大利亚加强了生物安全措施,包括农场保障、岸鸟测试和脆弱物种的 暴发,尽管 暴发 在世界各地的鸟类和哺乳动物中广泛存在,但对人类的全球风险评估为较低。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-08-03;事件报道于2026年8月3日。
地点与对象
澳大利亚,阿德莱德以南海岸(南澳大利亚州贾法角)和国家家禽养殖场。
事件
农业部长朱莉·柯林斯 (Julie Collins) 报道并 StratNews Global 报道,澳大利亚当局确认该国首次发生与 H5N1 禽流感 相关的大规模海鸟 死亡率 事件。
规模、影响与风险
大约 50 只大凤头燕鸥死亡;49只死亡燕鸥和35只患病燕鸥H5N1病毒检测呈阳性;澳大利亚全国共有 74 例 H5N1 确诊病例,分布在西澳大利亚州、昆士兰州和南澳大利亚州;尽管鸟类和哺乳动物在全球范围内传播,但对人类的风险被描述为较低。
应对、状态与不确定性
澳大利亚家禽养殖场没有H5N1的证据;强化生物安全措施,包括农场生物安全、岸鸟测试、脆弱物种疫苗接种以及应对计划兵棋推演;官员警告野生动物传播范围扩大,并预计发生更大规模的 死亡率 事件。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Australia Confirms First Mass Seabird Deaths Linked To H5N1 Bird Flu - StratNews Global

News Brief
Australia confirmed its first mass seabird die-off linked to H5N1 avian influenza on August 3, 2026, after testing detected the virus in 49 dead and 35 sick greater crested terns along the coast south of Adelaide. The national tally of 74 confirmed H5N1 cases spans multiple states, though poultry farms remain unaffected. Agriculture Minister Julie Collins warned that wider wildlife spread is likely and that significant losses are expected. Australia has intensified biosecurity measures, including farm safeguards, shore bird testing, and vaccination of vulnerable species, while global risk to humans is assessed as low despite widespread outbreaks in birds and mammals worldwide.

StratNews Global World News Australia China Watch Indo-Pacific Asia Africa Europe North America South America West Asia Russia Neighbours Bhutan China Nepal Pakistan Afghanistan Sri Lanka Myanmar Maldives Videos Documentaries Support Us Home Australia Australia Confirms First Mass Seabird Deaths Linked To H5N1 Bird Flu Australia Confirms First Mass Seabird Deaths Linked To H5N1 Bird Flu By Team StratNews - August 3, 2026 Select Preferred on Google News Discover more Death & Tragedy Birds Bird Australia on Monday warned of the potential for a wider spread of the H5N1 bird flu virus after the country’s first mass seabird die-off claimed around 50 greater crested terns along the coast south of Adelaide. Authorities said there is still no evidence that the virus has reached Australia’s poultry farms, many of which have been under strict biosecurity measures since H5N1 was first detected in the country in June. Agriculture Minister Julie Collins said testing confirmed H5N1 bird flu in a group of 49 dead and 35 sick terns found by helicopter surveillance on rocks off Cape Jaffa, 250 km (155 miles) from Adelaide on Friday. Officials Warn of Wider Wildlife Spread “Australians should expect to see more spread and larger numbers now of wildlife being affected,” she told reporters in Canberra, the capital. “This is the first confirmed case of a mass mortality,” she added. “Once the H5 bird flu is spreading in wildlife and the natural environment, it is not possible to avoid significant losses, which is what we’re starting to see now.” Australia’s tally of 74 confirmed cases is spread as wide as the states of Western Australia and Queensland, though most cases were in South Australia, Collins said. Biosecurity Measures Intensified Australia and New Zealand – which saw its first H5N1 case last month – have spent years bracing for the virus, with steps such as tighter biosecurity at farms, testing of shore birds, vaccinating vulnerable species and war-gaming response plans. The virus has killed hundreds of millions of birds and mammals in recent years, causing billions of dollars in losses to poultry farmers, infecting cattle in the United States and littering beaches with corpses of birds and seals. H5N1 bird flu can infect humans who have contact with infected animals. But the number of cases worldwide is small and officials say the risk to humans is low. Australia, coast south of Adelaide (Cape Jaffa, South Australia), and national poultry farms.
Event
Australian authorities confirmed the country’s first mass seabird mortality event linked to H5N1 avian influenza, reported by Agriculture Minister Julie Collins and covered by StratNews Global.
Scale, impact and risk
Approximately 50 greater crested terns died; 49 dead and 35 sick terns tested positive for H5N1; Australia’s national tally of 74 confirmed H5N1 cases spans Western Australia, Queensland, and South Australia; risk to humans is described as low despite global spread in birds and mammals.
Response, status and uncertainty
No evidence of H5N1 in Australian poultry farms; biosecurity measures intensified including farm biosecurity, shore bird testing, vaccination of vulnerable species, and response plan war-gaming; officials warn of wider wildlife spread and expect larger mortality events.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

“我们预料到了,但现在它来了”:在澳大利亚禽流感 暴发 的前线,人们很痛苦 - 《卫报》

‘We expected it, but now it’s here’: on the frontline of Australia’s bird flu outbreak, people are distressed - The Guardian
新闻简报自2026年7月初以来,澳大利亚在南澳大利亚石灰岩海岸沿线的野生海鸟中确认了首例H5禽流感死亡率,其中有数十例疑似和确诊病例,并在博丹岩确认了大规模死亡率事件。暴发 已蔓延到维多利亚州,波特兰和尼尔森附近的空中和地面 暴发 显示死亡燕鸥明显增加。受威胁的物种,如仙女燕鸥和海洋哺乳动物,包括澳大利亚海狮和长鼻海狗,被认为是高度脆弱的。当地社区和野生动物护理人员报告称,普遍存在痛苦,病鸟和死鸟的数量有所增加,而野生动物诊所面临能力限制并实施严格的生物安全措施。当局敦促公众避免、记录和报告病鸟或死鸟,但强调灾难的真实程度以及对数百种本地物种的长期影响仍不确定。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-08-06;该事件始于 2026 年 7 月上旬,首次在当地海鸟中发现,并在过去一周升级。
地点与对象
南澳大利亚州的石灰岩海岸,特别是鹈鹕角 (Pelican Point)、博丹岩 (Baudin Rocks)(距离罗布 8 公里)、卡彭特岩 (Carpenter Rocks) 以及罗布和波特兰等沿海城镇;受影响的物种包括大凤头燕鸥、银鸥、仙女燕鸥、企鹅、海豚、海狮、长鼻海狗、袋熊、小袋鼠、针鼹以及鸻和牡蛎捕鸟等滨鸟。
事件
澳大利亚首次在野生海鸟中确认 H5 禽流感 暴发,目前正在多个物种中传播;确认在 Baudin Rocks 发生大规模 死亡率 事件;当地社区和野生动物护理人员报告了痛苦和病鸟/死鸟目击事件的增加;州政府建议“避免、记录和报告”;联邦环境部长将正在发生的事件描述为“可能是一个漫长而艰难的旅程的开始”。
规模、影响与风险
南澳州出现数十例疑似和确诊病例;确认在 Baudin Rocks 发生大规模 死亡率 事件;据报道,维多利亚州波特兰和尼尔森附近的燕鸥死亡数量有所增加;多种被确定为高风险的受威胁物种,包括仙燕鸥;海洋哺乳动物(澳大利亚海狮、长鼻海狗)被认为是最脆弱的;野生动物诊所不堪重负且已满员;未收集的尸体造成环境污染的风险,对拾荒者构成感染风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
南澳大利亚州第一产业部门监测和检测;州政府建议公众避免、记录和报告病鸟或死鸟;野生动物护理人员和诊所实施个人防护装备和检疫措施;呼吁更加重视减缓传播;联邦和州官员监测情况;对数百种本地物种和人类接触途径的长期影响仍然存在不确定性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

‘We expected it, but now it’s here’: on the frontline of Australia’s bird flu outbreak, people are distressed - The Guardian

News Brief
Since early July 2026, Australia has confirmed its first H5 bird flu outbreak in wild seabirds along the Limestone Coast in South Australia, with dozens of suspected and confirmed cases and a confirmed mass mortality event at Baudin Rocks. The outbreak has spread to Victoria, where aerial and ground surveillance near Portland and Nelson shows a clear increase in dead terns. Threatened species such as fairy terns and marine mammals including Australian sea lions and long-nosed fur seals are considered highly vulnerable. Local communities and wildlife carers report widespread distress and increased sightings of sick and dead birds, while wildlife clinics face capacity constraints and implement strict biosecurity measures. Authorities urge the public to avoid, record, and report sick or dead birds, but emphasize that the true extent of the disaster and long-term impacts on hundreds of native species remain uncertain.
Original source text (English)

Photograph: Veronica Miller View image in fullscreen A dead greater crested tern found at Pelican Point on the Limestone Coast in South Australia. Photograph: Veronica Miller ‘We expected it, but now it’s here’: on the frontline of Australia’s bird flu outbreak, people are distressed As Australia prepares for the H5 bird flu to potentially rip through hundreds of species, residents of the Limestone Coast are already seeing the impacts Follow our Australia news live blog for latest updates Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast It has only been three weeks since H5 bird flu was detected on South Australia’s Limestone Coast and already encounters with sick or dead birds are becoming commonplace. Maureen Christie, who lives in the seaside town of Carpenter Rocks, has been observing and protecting birds in the region for decades. She is now witnessing many stricken and suffering. “It’s just awful,” Christie said, who helped establish the Friends of Shorebirds SE more than 20 years ago. “We expected it but, now it’s here, it’s just traumatic.” The state’s south-east is renowned for its bird life, she said, which includes resident hooded plovers and oyster catchers, along with fly-ins from other countries, such as double-banded plovers, red-necked stints, sanderlings and ruddy turnstones. Sign up for the Breaking News Australia email Locals have been following state government advice to “avoid, record and report”, she said. Christie said many people have been getting in touch by email and phone to relay their own sightings and reports. “Everybody’s feeling distressed, but everybody’s trying to be supportive of one another.” Since the first case in a local Australian seabird – a greater crested tern – was detected in early July, the region has seen dozens more suspected and confirmed cases, including the first confirmed mass mortality event at Baudin Rocks, a group of islets about 8km from the coastal town of Robe. “Robe has a very special and unique environment that the local community knows, uses, celebrates and cares for,” the president of Friends of Little Dip Conservation Park and Robe Coast Care, Roland Wells, said. There’s everything from migratory, coastal and inland birds, wombats, wallabies and echidnas, as well as marine mammals such as sea lions, seals and dolphins. He said people have undertaken conservation work over many decades to support shorebirds, which makes the recent arrival of bird flu “very upsetting”. The situation has escalated considerably over the past week. Council officers from Robe district council have been identifying and reporting sick and deceased birds almost daily, mainly greater crested terns and some silver gulls. Penguins and dolphins among nearly 400 Australian species under threat from bird flu as ‘true extent of disaster’ revealed Read more A spokesperson for South Australia’s primary industries department said crested terns – which make up many of the bird flu cases – “commonly commingle” with other species like cormorants, gulls, grebes, ducks and other tern species. This increases the potential for transmission. “From a conservation perspective, several threatened and priority species have been identified as high risk, including fairy terns,” the spokesperson said. “Marine mammals are also being closely monitored, with Australian sea lions and long-nosed fur seals among the species considered most vulnerable to potential impacts from the virus.” Wildlife carers and clinics – now required to don personal protective equipment and undertake strict cleaning and quarantine measures when dealing with other wild birds injured by cars, in dog and cat attacks or entanglement – have been calling on the state government for support. Mish Simpson, from SKER wildlife veterinary centre, said the clinic was already at capacity before bird flu. But now, more wild birds are being brought in because other vets have stopped accepting them, partly due to the additional requirements and the risk of exposure for other animals. Simpson is concerned there is not enough emphasis on slowing the spread, which would give vets, carers and the community more time to prepare. For example, she said, the dead bodies of infected birds are often being left in the environment, where they pose an infection risk to other wildlife, particularly scavengers. As the federal environment minister, Murray Watt, observed on Monday, the events rapidly unfolding in the south-east, and around Australia, are “the beginning of what is likely to be a long, difficult journey in terms of the impacts on wildlife”. In Victoria, the chief veterinary officer, Dr Graeme Cooke, said recent aerial and ground surveillance near Portland and Nelson has already shown “a clear increase in the number of dead terns along that part of the coast”, consistent with deaths in South Australia. “Last week we talked about individual detections; the first cases of the H5 bird flu in Victoria. We are now seeing a change.” What that means for hundreds of native species and the people who care for them remains uncertain. View five news elements
Time
Published 2026-08-06; event began in early July 2026 with first detection in a local seabird and escalated over the past week.
Location and population
South Australia’s Limestone Coast, particularly Pelican Point, Baudin Rocks (8 km from Robe), Carpenter Rocks, and coastal towns including Robe and Portland; affected species include greater crested terns, silver gulls, fairy terns, penguins, dolphins, sea lions, long-nosed fur seals, wombats, wallabies, echidnas, and shorebirds such as hooded plovers and oyster catchers.
Event
Australia’s first confirmed H5 bird flu outbreak in wild seabirds, now spreading among multiple species; confirmed mass mortality event at Baudin Rocks; local communities and wildlife carers report distress and increased sick/dead bird sightings; state government advises 'avoid, record and report'; federal environment minister describes unfolding events as 'the beginning of what is likely to be a long, difficult journey'.
Scale, impact and risk
Dozens of suspected and confirmed cases in South Australia; confirmed mass mortality event at Baudin Rocks; increased dead terns reported in Victoria near Portland and Nelson; multiple threatened species identified as high risk including fairy terns; marine mammals (Australian sea lions, long-nosed fur seals) considered most vulnerable; wildlife clinics overwhelmed and at capacity; risk of environmental contamination from uncollected carcasses posing infection risk to scavengers.
Response, status and uncertainty
South Australia’s primary industries department monitoring and testing; state government advises public to avoid, record, and report sick or dead birds; wildlife carers and clinics implementing PPE and quarantine measures; calls for greater emphasis on slowing spread; federal and state officials monitoring situation; uncertainty remains about long-term impacts on hundreds of native species and human exposure pathways.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

专家警告说,南澳大利亚H5N1禽流感病毒暴发的增长对濒临灭绝的海狮构成威胁 - 星报

Growing outbreak of H5N1 bird flu in South Australia a threat to endangered sea lions, expert warns - The Star
新闻简报一位专家表示,南澳大利亚 H5N1 禽流感病毒 暴发 的数量不断增加,引发了人们对其可能传播给濒临灭绝的澳大利亚海狮的担忧。暴发 于 6 月首次发现,已导致该州出现 40 例确诊或疑似病例,自 7 月 31 日星期一以来报告了 33 例。在海豹湾发现了 1 例病例,宿主 约占全球濒危物种数量的 5%。H5N1病毒株此前曾导致南美洲海狮和澳大利亚南象海豹幼崽大规模死亡。南澳大利亚州政府已暂停海豹湾的海滩旅游,以保护当地居民。官员们强调,没有证据表明该病毒存在于农业或家禽系统中,对人类健康的威胁仍然很低。政府正在与专家合作遏制疫情传播,但尚未详细说明检测或接触者追踪等具体措施。
查看新闻五要素
时间
据报道,截至 2026 年 8 月 3 日,暴发 在南澳大利亚呈增长趋势,该州已有 40 例确诊或疑似病例,其中 33 例是自周一(2026 年 7 月 31 日)以来报告的。
地点与对象
南澳大利亚州,特别是东南海岸和袋鼠岛,包括海豹湾,这里栖息着约占全球濒临灭绝的澳大利亚海狮数量的 5%。
事件
据报道,南澳大利亚 H5N1 禽流感病例不断增加,专家警告说,在海鸥中发现了疑似病例,人们担心其可能传播到濒临灭绝的海狮。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年7月31日,南澳州有确诊病例19例,疑似病例21例,病例总数40例。H5N1 毒株此前曾在南美海狮和澳大利亚南象海豹幼崽中造成严重的 死亡率。对人类健康的威胁被认为很低,并且没有证据表明该病毒存在于农业或家禽系统中。
应对、状态与不确定性
南澳大利亚州政府暂时中止了海豹湾的海滩旅游,以保护当地的海狮和海豹种群。官员们正在与专家合作遏制病毒的传播,但尚未提供有关检测、隔离或接触者追踪的进一步细节。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Growing outbreak of H5N1 bird flu in South Australia a threat to endangered sea lions, expert warns - The Star

News Brief
A growing outbreak of H5N1 bird flu in South Australia has raised concerns about its potential spread to endangered Australian sea lions, according to an expert. The outbreak, first detected in June, has led to 40 confirmed or suspected cases in the state, with 33 reported since Monday, July 31. One case was found at Seal Bay, which hosts about five per cent of the global population of the endangered species. The H5N1 strain has previously caused mass deaths in sea lions in South America and southern elephant seal pups in Australia. The South Australian government has suspended beach tours at Seal Bay to protect the local population. Officials emphasize that there is no evidence of the virus in agricultural or poultry systems, and the threat to human health remains low. The government is collaborating with experts to contain the spread, but no specific measures like testing or contact tracing have been detailed.
Original source text (English)

Rugby 15h ago Rugby-Eddie Jones rings the changes as Japan seek maiden win... CANBERRA: A growing outbreak of H5N1 avian influenza in South Australia (SA) will "almost certainly" spread to an endangered species of sea lion, an expert has warned. Authorities in SA reported that Australia's first suspected case of the highly pathogenic strain in a seagull has been detected on the state's south-east coast. It takes the total number of suspected cases of the strain in SA to 21 in addition to 19 confirmed cases. As of Friday (July 31), the rest of Australia's states and territories had reported 14 confirmed cases of the strain, which was first detected on the mainland in June. Of the 40 confirmed or suspected cases in SA, 33 have been reported since Monday, including four that were detected on Kangaroo Island off the state's south coast. One of the Kangaroo Island cases was detected at Seal Bay, which is home to about five per cent of the global population of the endangered Australian sea lion. Wayne Boardman, a wildlife veterinarian from the University of Adelaide, told the Australian Broadcasting Corporation on Friday that historical events in South America have shown that sea lions and fur seals are particularly susceptible to the strain. StarPicks Malaysia Madani’s fuel subsidy helps economic and social development "The issue now with bird flu being found on Seal Bay is that it's almost certainly going to go from the birds into the sea lions," he said. The H5N1 strain killed an estimated 30,000 sea lions in South America within six months in 2022 and killed an estimated 13,359 southern elephant seal pups on Australia's Heard Island in the Southern Ocean between August 2025 and January 2026. The SA government on Wednesday announced that beach tours on Seal Bay would be temporarily suspended to protect local sea lion and seal populations. Minister for Agriculture, Fisheries and Forestry Julie Collins told reporters on Friday that the government would continue working with experts to contain the spread of the strain. She said there has been no evidence of the virus in agricultural or poultry systems and the threat to human health remains low. - Xinhua Follow us on our official WhatsApp channel for breaking news alerts and key updates! B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

《卫报》揭示了“灾难的真实程度”,近 400 种澳大利亚物种中的企鹅和海豚受到禽流感的威胁

Penguins and dolphins among nearly 400 Australian species under threat from bird flu as ‘true extent of disaster’ revealed - The Guardian
新闻简报A federal government assessment, conducted with Charles Darwin University and BirdLife Australia, has revealed that nearly 400 native Australian bird and mammal species are at high risk from the deadly H5N1 bird flu virus. The assessment, released on Tuesday evening, highlights that almost one in five Australian mammals and birds face severe population reductions or extinction. The virus was confirmed in South Australia six weeks ago, following its spread from Europe in 2021. Five species are considered at extreme risk, including the Tasmanian devil and eastern quoll. The government has allocated $113m to prepare for bird flu, but conservation groups are urging more funding to protect wildlife. The findings underscore the growing threat to Australia's biodiversity and the need for urgent action.
查看新闻五要素
时间
联邦政府于周二晚间发布了评估报告,该病毒在六周前传播到澳大利亚,并在 100 多只鸟类中得到证实,并出现了南澳大利亚州首次大规模 死亡率 事件(N1、N28、N29)。
地点与对象
澳大利亚,特别是西澳大利亚州、南澳大利亚州、维多利亚州、新南威尔士州和昆士兰州,有近 400 种本土鸟类和哺乳动物物种面临危险,包括企鹅、海豚、海狗、袋鼬和袋獾(N1、N3、N12、N22、N24)。
事件
联邦政府的一项评估显示,近 400 种澳大利亚本土物种面临 H5N1 禽流感的高风险,其中 5 种被认为面临极高风险。该评估是与查尔斯达尔文大学和澳大利亚鸟类保护协会(N3、N8、N12、N13、N21)合作进行的。
规模、影响与风险
评估发现,澳大利亚近五分之一的哺乳动物和鸟类面临数量严重减少或灭绝的风险。塔斯马尼亚袋獾和东袋鼬等一些物种处于高风险,而有 5 个物种处于极端风险(N5、N7、N9、N13、N21)。
应对、状态与不确定性
The government has committed $113m to prepare for bird flu, including $47m for protective action for threatened species. However, conservation groups are calling for increased funding, and the government has not yet confirmed the full extent of the outbreak or its impact on wildlife (N24, N25, N23).
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Penguins and dolphins among nearly 400 Australian species under threat from bird flu as ‘true extent of disaster’ revealed - The Guardian

News Brief
A federal government assessment, conducted with Charles Darwin University and BirdLife Australia, has revealed that nearly 400 native Australian bird and mammal species are at high risk from the deadly H5N1 bird flu virus. The assessment, released on Tuesday evening, highlights that almost one in five Australian mammals and birds face severe population reductions or extinction. The virus was confirmed in South Australia six weeks ago, following its spread from Europe in 2021. Five species are considered at extreme risk, including the Tasmanian devil and eastern quoll. The government has allocated $113m to prepare for bird flu, but conservation groups are urging more funding to protect wildlife. The findings underscore the growing threat to Australia's biodiversity and the need for urgent action.

Photograph: leelakajonkij/Getty Images/iStockphoto View image in fullscreen Little penguins are among numerous species at very high risk from deadly H5N1, a federal government assessment has revealed. Photograph: leelakajonkij/Getty Images/iStockphoto Penguins and dolphins among nearly 400 Australian species under threat from bird flu as ‘true extent of disaster’ revealed Assessment of more than 2,000 native species warns nearly one in five at high risk of deadly H5N1, including parrots, fur seals and quolls Follow our Australia news live blog for latest updates Sign up for climate and environment editor Adam Morton’s free Clear Air newsletter here Almost 400 native Australian bird and mammal species have been assessed as being at a high risk – or worse – from the deadly H5N1 bird flu virus, a federal government assessment has revealed. Some of Australia’s most beloved animals – including species of penguins, fur seals, quolls, dolphins, more than 300 birds and the Tasmanian devil – are considered at least high risk from the virus, meaning they could suffer “severe reduction in population size or viability” or worse if it reached them. Five species are considered at extreme risk, meaning “unlikely to survive where the virus occurs”. Suspected mass bird flu deaths found in second state as Murray Watt warns ‘this is the beginning’ Read more These are all birds that breed in single islands or groups of islands, such as the endangered Christmas Island frigatebird and the critically endangered herald petrel, which only breeds on Raine Island in the far north of the Great Barrier Reef. More than 80 endangered and critically endangered species are also considered to be facing at least a high risk from an infection, suggesting that the H5N1 strain of bird flu will push them further towards extinction. The federal government released the results of the assessment of more than 2,000 native species, carried out with Charles Darwin University and BirdLife Australia, on Tuesday evening. Nearly one in five have been rated as facing extreme, very high or high risk. View image in fullscreen Australian sea lions, whose populations have declined by 60% in four decades, are at very high risk of H5N1. Photograph: Brad Leue/Alamy Australia’s three resident seals – the Australian fur seal, the New Zealand fur seal and the Australian sea lion – are all considered at very high risk. The Tasmanian devil and the eastern quoll, both assessed as high risk, are the land mammals at greatest threat. The extent of the analysis and the number of species considered at high risk underlines expert fears for the threat to local wildlife. Sign up to get climate and environment editor Adam Morton’s Clear Air column as a free newsletter Prof John Woinarski, a leading conservation scientist at Charles Darwin University who helped devise the analysis, said: “This has sketched out a more bleak picture than perhaps we considered previously. “We are going to get major consequences for many species and our bird and mammal diversity is likely to suffer substantial impacts from this disease.” View image in fullscreen Tasmanian devils and eastern quolls are the land mammals at greatest threat. Photograph: Eric J Woehler Prof Hugh Possingham of the University of Queensland, a lead councillor at the Biodiversity Council, said the analysis was “a good first cut” but it should have been released two years ago. “A year ago we needed a priority action list and we should have been taking action then because everyone knew that it was coming,” he said. Kate Millar, the chief executive of BirdLife Australia, said while the list of species at high risk or worse was “long” and “incredibly confronting” it also meant Australia could “prioritise and take action protecting the species most at risk”. Jack Gough, the chief executive of the Invasive Species Council, said the “list of doom” revealed “the true extent of the disaster facing Australian wildlife”. He said it demanded a “serious response” from the government “but instead we have had cuts to on-ground environment programs nationally”. “Almost one in five Australian mammals and birds has been rated by the government as at risk of severe population reductions or even extinction,” he said. “This includes quolls, parrots, seals and pelicans.” View image in fullscreen The critically endangered orange-bellied parrot is among several parrot species at high risk of bird flu. Photograph: Minden Pictures/Alamy The council is among several conservation groups calling for the government to fund a $200m package to scale up on-ground efforts to protect wildlife, including feral animal controls. The government has said it has committed $113m to prepare for bird flu, including $47m “to accelerate protective action” for threatened species and biodiversity. The environment minister, Murray Watt, said the risk analysis had already informed investments to protect captive animal populations, removing feral threats and inoculating against more common diseases. “We have been investing in these types of projects across the country for several years in preparation for a bird flu outbreak, and as a result, our native species are in a better position to withstand the H5 incursion.” Jess Abrahams, national nature campaigner at the Australian Conservation Foundation, said: “Australia is already in the midst of an extinction crisis and we need to be prepared for just how harrowing this could get. “Australians love nature and we all need to be emotionally prepared for the onslaught that could occur and know what to do if they come across a sick, dying or dead animal.” The H5N1 virus has killed millions of birds and mammals since it spread from Europe in 2021 to every continent on the planet. Australia was the final continent to be breached six weeks ago, when infected Antarctic and subantarctic migratory seabirds were found dead and sick in Western Australia and South Australia. The virus has now been confirmed in more than 100 individual birds and air surveillance has recorded the first mass mortality event from the disease, in South Australia. Detections have also been confirmed in Victoria, New South Wales and Queensland. The federal government released the assessment on Tuesday evening, with the virus spreading to Australia six weeks prior and confirmed in more than 100 individual birds and South Australia's first mass mortality event (N1, N28, N29).

Australia, specifically mentioning Western Australia, South Australia, Victoria, New South Wales, and Queensland, with nearly 400 native bird and mammal species at risk, including penguins, dolphins, fur seals, quolls, and the Tasmanian devil (N1, N3, N12, N22, N24).

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

澳大利亚首例大规模 死亡率 事件疑似致命禽流感病毒,在本土鸟类中迅速升级 - 《卫报》

First Australian mass mortality event suspected as deadly bird flu virus rapidly escalates among native birds - The Guardian
新闻简报澳大利亚报告了疑似 H5N1 禽流感 死亡率,这是本土鸟类中首次发生大规模 死亡率 事件。南澳大利亚海岸发现了超过 80 只死亡和垂死的大凤头燕鸥,其中 49 只确诊死亡,35 只患病。另外三只燕鸥和银鸥疑似呈阳性,表明病毒正在内陆传播。家禽养殖场所在地莫纳托地区和维多利亚州也报告了病例,该病毒目前在多个地区存在。南澳大利亚州首席兽医 Skye Fruean 指出,这种传播很难阻止,但 监测 仍在持续。暴发 引起了人们对澳大利亚海狮和其他野生动物影响的担忧,因为众所周知,银鸥会清除和传播这种病毒。专家警告称,局势可能进一步升级,南美和欧洲也出现了类似的 暴发 事件。目前情况仍在调查中,目前还没有确定的方法来阻止传播。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 1 日报告了疑似 H5N1 禽流感集体 死亡率 事件,六周前在澳大利亚发现了第一例病例。该事件迅速升级,几天内病例数增加了 20 例,总数达到 53 例。
地点与对象
该事件发生在南澳大利亚海岸附近,特别是博丹岩、罗布和家禽养殖场所在地莫纳托地区附近。它涉及更大的凤头燕鸥和银鸥,人们担心病毒会传播到濒危物种澳大利亚海狮。
事件
澳大利亚本土鸟类中发现疑似 H5N1 禽流感集体 死亡率 事件,博丹岩石上发现 49 只大凤头燕鸥死亡和 35 只患病燕鸥。其他燕鸥和银鸥也报告了三例疑似阳性病例,表明病毒正在内陆传播。
规模、影响与风险
据报道,有超过 80 只大凤头燕鸥死亡和垂死,银鸥中还有其他疑似阳性病例。该病毒已传播到澳大利亚多个地区,包括维多利亚州、西澳大利亚州、新南威尔士州和昆士兰州。由于该病毒可能影响大量种群,因此凸显了进一步传播到其他鸟类和野生动物(包括澳大利亚海狮)的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
南澳大利亚州首席兽医 Skye Fruean 表示,“我们无能为力”来阻止病毒传播,但 监测 的努力仍在继续。澳大利亚联邦科学与工业研究组织(CSIRO)确认了首例银鸥病例,目前正在进行进一步测试。目前情况仍在调查中,专家对病毒可能升级的可能性表示担忧。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

First Australian mass mortality event suspected as deadly bird flu virus rapidly escalates among native birds - The Guardian

News Brief
A suspected H5N1 bird flu outbreak has been reported in Australia, marking the first mass mortality event among native birds. Over 80 dead and dying greater crested terns were found off the South Australian coast, with 49 confirmed dead and 35 sick. Three other terns and silver gulls were suspected positive, indicating the virus is spreading inland. The Monarto area, home to poultry farms, and Victoria have also reported cases, with the virus now present in multiple regions. South Australia’s chief vet, Skye Fruean, noted the spread is difficult to stop, but surveillance is ongoing. The outbreak has raised concerns about the impact on Australian sea lions and other wildlife, as silver gulls are known to scavenge and spread the virus. Experts warn of the potential for further escalation, mirroring similar outbreaks in South America and Europe. The situation remains under investigation, with no confirmed methods to halt the spread.
Original source text (English)

Photograph: imageBROKER/David & Micha Sheldon/Getty Images/imageBROKER RF View image in fullscreen More than 80 dead and dying greater crested terns have been spotted off the South Australian coast in what is a suspected H5N1 bird flu mass mortality event. Photograph: imageBROKER/David & Micha Sheldon/Getty Images/imageBROKER RF First Australian mass mortality event suspected as deadly bird flu virus rapidly escalates among native birds Helicopter surveillance finds dead and dying greater crested terns off South Australian coast as H5 virus spreads Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast More than 80 dead and sick birds seen on rocks off the South Australian coast are suspected to be the first mass mortality event from the deadly H5N1 bird flu virus, only six weeks after the disease arrived in Australia. Authorities reported on Saturday that helicopter surveillance had revealed 49 dead greater crested terns on Baudin Rocks, near Robe, as well as 35 more sick terns. While some of the birds had been collected for testing, South Australia’s chief vet Skye Fruean said it was likely the first mass mortality event from the disease. Sign up for the Breaking News Australia email South Australia also reported three suspected positive cases in other greater crested terns, including one inland at Monarto, suggesting the disease has begun moving away from the coast. The Monarto area is home to several poultry farms. Three silver gulls were also suspected positive, SA authorities said, adding to the first silver gull found a few days ago and confirmed positive by the CSIRO earlier on Saturday. Kate Millar, the chief executive of conservation charity BirdLife Australia, called it a “very significant escalation of the bird flu situation in Australia, and a very bleak milestone”. “Sadly, we’re at the beginning of a long and challenging battle and birds and wildlife are going to need all the help they can get,” she said. Jack Gough, the chief executive of the Invasive Species Council, said it now appeared the disease was “starting to take off” and “mirroring the experience in South America of early detections, then rapid increases in numbers and spread leading to mass mortality events in wildlife.” “We are still at the very early stages of this devastating disease spreading, but the possible first mass mortality coming just days after the virus was declared not eradicable indicates how quickly this could escalate,” he said. Fruean said there was “nothing we can do” to stop the spread of the disease, but that surveillance was working. The news came at the end of a significant week in the development of the disease when numbers jumped by 20 in just a few days to the current total of 53. Australia was the last continent to have the unwelcome arrival of a disease that has killed millions of birds and mammals around the world. Victoria this week confirmed its first cases of the virulent strain in a greater crested tern found in Portland, with the state joining Western Australia, South Australia, New South Wales and Queensland that have all recorded positive cases. On Saturday, Victorian authorities said samples from five crested terns had been sent to the CSIRO laboratory in Geelong, with preliminary testing suggesting more positive results. Five confirmed cases in birds at Seal Bay on Kangaroo Island also raised concerns for the welfare of the area’s Australian sea lions – an endangered species. Tens of thousands of seals have died worldwide from the disease. But it has been the cases among silver gulls that has experts like Sean Dooley, the national affairs adviser at BirdLife Australia, concerned that the spread of the virus has now reached a critical new stage. “When people think of a seagull, they’re almost always thinking of the silver gull,” said Dooley. skip past newsletter promotion after newsletter promotion View image in fullscreen Australian sea lions, an endangered species, are at risk of contracting H5N1 bird flu. Photograph: Brad Leue/Alamy “They’re very common, and primarily they are coastal, but they do come inland and can turn up in Lake Eyre following floods,” said Dooley. Silver gulls are the second Australian native species to catch the disease, after dozens of detections in greater crested terns. But it is the silver gull’s behaviour and habits that have experts concerned because they scavenge from dead or dying animals, meaning they can easily pick up the disease from other sick birds. They also pick detritus from water bodies – lakes and flooded areas included – and these are known to be breeding grounds for the disease. “There is such a large population of silver gulls, and they move into other habitats. They could be the vector that takes bird flu inland,” says Dooley. “They’re also really successful in urban areas and could bring the disease into those bird populations.” Because Australia has a high concentration of unique birds, the susceptibility of many native species isn’t known. But the fears are there is no reason to think they would manage any better than birds overseas. Birds that flock around water have tended to be the hardest hit. Examples include sandwich terns, black-headed gulls and northern gannets in Europe and, in Peru, pelicans. Dooley said the mood at BirdLife Australia this week had been one of “trying to suppress that sinking, gut-churning feeling”. The winter season for birdwatchers along the coast had been one of the best in 10 years, said Dooley, with rare petrels turning up in numbers along the east coast. “Now that has been heavily tinged because probably among those were birds carrying H5N1.” Explore more on these topics Bird flu Wildlife Birds Marine life Animals news Share Reuse this content Most viewed ACLU blasts Trump’s new orders on birthright citizenship, saying they are doomed to fail in court– as it happened US federal judge halts order for Trump to turn over financial records to BBC US law enforcement gave false information in case against woman charged in anti-ICE protest Miss North Carolina USA stripped of title after alleged racist posts resurface Trump again rebukes Jeanine Pirro over reflecting pool ‘vandalism’ case Most viewed Most viewed
View five news elements
Time
The suspected H5N1 bird flu mass mortality event was reported on August 1, 2026, with the first cases identified in Australia six weeks prior. The event escalated rapidly, with numbers jumping by 20 in a few days to reach a total of 53 cases.
Location and population
The event occurred off the South Australian coast, specifically near Baudin Rocks, Robe, and the Monarto area, which is home to poultry farms. It involved greater crested terns and silver gulls, with concerns about the spread to Australian sea lions, an endangered species.
Event
A suspected H5N1 bird flu mass mortality event was identified among native birds in Australia, with 49 dead greater crested terns and 35 sick ones found on Baudin Rocks. Three suspected positive cases were also reported in other terns and silver gulls, indicating the virus is spreading inland.
Scale, impact and risk
Over 80 dead and dying greater crested terns were reported, with additional suspected positive cases in silver gulls. The virus has spread to multiple regions in Australia, including Victoria, Western Australia, New South Wales, and Queensland. The risk of further spread to other bird species and wildlife, including Australian sea lions, is highlighted due to the virus's potential to affect large populations.
Response, status and uncertainty
South Australia’s chief vet, Skye Fruean, stated that there was "nothing we can do" to stop the spread, but surveillance efforts are ongoing. The CSIRO confirmed the first silver gull case, and further testing is being conducted. The situation remains under investigation, with experts expressing concern about the virus's potential to escalate.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Australia, New Zealand records more H5N1 cases in wild birds - WATTPoultry.com

News Brief
Australia and New Zealand have recorded more cases of H5N1 in wild birds, according to a report from WATTPoultry.com. The article also mentions additional outbreaks in poultry in Asia, highlighting the ongoing presence of highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) in North America. The report notes that HPAI remains endemic in waterfowl and other wildlife, and the USDA is considering future vaccination plans. However, no specific case or death counts are provided. The situation is under investigation, and the article does not detail current containment or mitigation efforts. The report serves as a summary of recent developments in avian influenza, emphasizing the need for continued monitoring and preparedness.

“末日清单”:禽流感使澳大利亚五分之一的鸟类和哺乳动物面临危险 - SMH.com.au

‘List of doom’: Bird flu puts one in five Australian bird and mammal species at risk - SMH.com.au
新闻简报A report from the Australian federal environment department highlights that almost one in five bird and mammal species in Australia are at high, very high, or extreme risk from the bird flu virus, particularly H5N1. The virus is spreading across the continent, with five bird species, including the Christmas Island frigate bird and Houtman albatross, assessed as being at extreme risk of extinction. High-risk species include the Tasmanian devil, eastern quoll, and seals, while the common dolphin is at very high risk. The risk assessment considers both the species' susceptibility to the virus and their conservation status. Environment Minister Murray Watt confirmed local transmission in South Australia, noting that $113 million has been allocated for surveillance and response. However, critics argue that these measures are inadequate and that the government has not provided sufficient transparency or funding for conservation efforts. The report was released late, raising concerns about the preparedness of Australia's wildlife protection programs.
查看新闻五要素
时间
打开辅助功能指南跳到部分导航跳到内容跳到页脚登录我们的网络登录展开导航菜单搜索广告环境保护禽流感“末日清单”:禽流感使澳大利亚五分之一的鸟类和哺乳动物面临风险尼克·奥马利2026年8月5日 - 凌晨5:00保存登录、注册或订阅以保存文章供以后使用。
地点与对象
澳大利亚,包括特定鸟类和哺乳动物物种,如圣诞岛军舰鸟、先驱海燕、霍特曼信天翁、赫德岛和麦格理岛的帝鹬、海狮、海狗、海象、塔斯马尼亚袋獾、东袋鼬和八种海豚,其中普通海豚处于极高风险。
事件
联邦环境部发布的一份报告表明,澳大利亚近五分之一的鸟类和哺乳动物物种面临禽流感病毒的高风险、极高风险或极高风险,尤其是正在整个大陆传播的 H5N1 病毒。该报告发布较晚,受到缺乏事先通知和保护工作资金不足的批评。
规模、影响与风险
该报告根据物种对疾病的易感性及其保护状况评估风险。有五种鸟类处于极度危险之中,可能面临灭绝。高风险物种包括塔斯马尼亚袋獾、东袋鼬和海狮,而极高风险物种包括普通海豚和几种企鹅。风险类别表明人口严重减少、非常严重的下降或灭绝。
应对、状态与不确定性
The federal government has committed $113 million to bird flu surveillance, preparedness, and response measures. However, there is criticism that these investments are insufficient and that the government has not provided transparency on how species and locations are being prioritized. The situation is under investigation, with concerns about the timing of the report and the adequacy of preparedness efforts.

‘List of doom’: Bird flu puts one in five Australian bird and mammal species at risk - SMH.com.au

News Brief
A report from the Australian federal environment department highlights that almost one in five bird and mammal species in Australia are at high, very high, or extreme risk from the bird flu virus, particularly H5N1. The virus is spreading across the continent, with five bird species, including the Christmas Island frigate bird and Houtman albatross, assessed as being at extreme risk of extinction. High-risk species include the Tasmanian devil, eastern quoll, and seals, while the common dolphin is at very high risk. However, critics argue that these measures are inadequate and that the government has not provided sufficient transparency or funding for conservation efforts. The report was released late, raising concerns about the preparedness of Australia's wildlife protection programs.

Open accessibility guideSkip to sections navigationSkip to contentSkip to footer Log in Our network Log in Expand navigation menuSearch Advertisement EnvironmentConservationBird flu ‘List of doom’: Bird flu puts one in five Australian bird and mammal species at risk Nick O'Malley August 5, 2026 — 5:00am Save Log in, register or subscribe to save articles for later. You have reached your maximum number of saved items. Remove items from your saved list to add more. Share AAA Add us as a preferred source on Google Listen to this article 5 min Advertisement Almost one in five Australian bird and mammal species is at high, very high or extreme risk from the bird flu virus now spreading in Australia, according to data published on the federal environment department’s website on Tuesday evening. Five bird species are assessed as being at extreme risk, meaning that they cannot survive where the virus occurs. They include the Christmas Island frigate bird, the Herald petrel, Houtman albatross, imperial shag on Heard Island and imperial shag on Macquarie Island. Christmas Island frigate birds are among the species at extreme risk.AP The highest-risk marine mammals are the sea lion, fur seal and elephant seal, and the highest risk land mammals are the Tasmanian devil and eastern quoll. Eight species of dolphin are at risk and one, the common dolphin, is at very high risk. The risk category of each animal has been calculated by assessing both the susceptibility of the animal to the disease and the animal’s conservation status. Animals with limited ranges or those that congregate in habitats known to be prone to the transmission of the H5N1 virus – such as colonies of waterbirds – are considered to be at higher risk. An animal categorised as at high risk faces severe reduction in population or viability, very high risk describes those facing very severe population decline, while those in the extreme risk category may face extinction. “Most Australian animals are found nowhere else in the world,” says the analysis. “This means the potential impacts of H5 bird flu are hard to predict.” The chief executive of the Invasive Species Council, Jack Gough, said he was shocked at the level of risk described in the document and expressed frustration at the way the data, which the environment sector has been waiting on for weeks, was issued late in the day without any notice. “This is a list of doom that demands a serious response, but instead we have had cuts to on-ground environment programs nationally,” he said. Advertisement From our partners Advertisement “The fact that they have got this list where almost one in five of Australia’s mammals and birds is at a risk of severe, or very severe population reduction or extinction, and they haven’t massively invested over the last few years in scaled-up protections for our wildlife is an indictment on the government. “To give you an idea of the sheer scale of this disaster, even the pelican and the ibis are at very high risk, all but one species of penguin is at high risk or greater than that. Even the cassowary is there. It is hard to grasp the scale of this tragedy, because it is so broad. Even with bushfires or the algal bloom they were geographically defined disasters. “This will hit all over the continent.” Video Player is loading. This modal can be closed by pressing the Escape key or activating the close button. 1:56 Authorities confirm local transmission of H5 bird flu Local transmission of bird flu has been confirmed in South Australia, marking a significant escalation in the outbreak. On Monday, Environment Minister Murray Watt confirmed that bird flu was transmitting in Australia after a mass death of terns in South Australia over the weekend was confirmed to have been caused by the H5N1 virus, which has already appeared on every other continent over the past five years, killing hundreds of millions of animals. He noted that $113 million had been committed by the federal government in bird flu surveillance, preparedness and response measures. But he said Australia was well-prepared, having learned lessons from watching the spread of the disease around the world and through investments in protecting vulnerable animals. This was challenged by the Biodiversity Council’s lead councillor, Professor Hugh Possingham from the University of Queensland. “If Australia’s wildlife is better prepared for H5 bird flu because of recent investments, we ask the government to report to Australians exactly what has been done and what difference it has made.” “Australia has 183 waterbird species, including 149 that congregate in large numbers and are therefore at greater risk of mass outbreaks. We also have 10 species of seals and sea lions that could be severely affected. “A handful of projects would not justify a claim that Australia’s wildlife is broadly better prepared for a threat of this scale. “The vast majority of conservation needs go unfunded, so we are asking the government to be transparent about how species, locations and actions are being prioritised, and how the outcomes were monitored and reported.” Workers dispose of dead cranes at the Hula Lake conservation area in northern Israel, where an outbreak killed more than 5000 of the migratory species.AP Another member of the council, Professor Stephen Garnett from Charles Darwin University, said the list was valuable, but was almost too late because the virus was already here. “Had the assessment been available earlier, we could have used it to guide preparation, such as testing vaccines on the most at-risk species.” Gough said even as the environmental sector was bracing for the impact of bird flu, environmental funding was cut in the last budget. Only by ramping up efforts to reduce pest species such as cats, foxes and pigs, and by protecting what is left of habitats, will some of these species survive the catastrophe, he said. Location and population
Australia, including specific bird and mammal species such as Christmas Island frigate bird, Herald petrel, Houtman albatross, imperial shag on Heard Island and Macquarie Island, sea lion, fur seal, elephant seal, Tasmanian devil, eastern quoll, and eight species of dolphin with the common dolphin at very high risk.
Event
A report published by the federal environment department indicates that almost one in five Australian bird and mammal species is at high, very high, or extreme risk from the bird flu virus, particularly H5N1, which is spreading across the continent. The report was released late, and there is criticism about the lack of prior notice and insufficient funding for conservation efforts.
Scale, impact and risk
The report assesses the risk based on both the susceptibility of the species to the disease and their conservation status. Five bird species are at extreme risk, potentially facing extinction. High-risk species include the Tasmanian devil, eastern quoll, and sea lions, while very high-risk species include the common dolphin and several penguin species. The risk categories indicate severe population reduction, very severe decline, or extinction.
Response, status and uncertainty
The federal government has committed $113 million to bird flu surveillance, preparedness, and response measures. However, there is criticism that these investments are insufficient and that the government has not provided transparency on how species and locations are being prioritized. The situation is under investigation, with concerns about the timing of the report and the adequacy of preparedness efforts.

随着病毒传播,敦促奶牛群应对禽流感 疫苗 - Farm Progress

Bird flu vaccine for dairy herds urged as virus spreads - Farm Progress
新闻简报The article discusses the spread of H5N1 avian influenza, which has transitioned from poultry to dairy cattle in the U.S. since March 2024. Over 1,000 herds across at least 19 states have been infected, causing a $4 billion economic impact on the dairy sector and an estimated $14 billion total loss, with over 190 million birds killed since 2022. The CDC has reported 71 human cases, including two deaths, primarily among farm workers. Gregory Gray from the University of Texas Medical Branch argues that vaccinating dairy herds could be the most critical step to prevent further spread and adaptation of the virus in mammals, which increases the risk to humans. While several vaccine candidates are in USDA field trials, none have been approved yet, and the effectiveness and timeline of vaccination remain under investigation. The article highlights the urgency of addressing the outbreak to mitigate its impact on both animal and human health.
查看新闻五要素
时间
由于该病毒于2024年3月进入奶牛体内,该文章于2026年8月6日发表。
地点与对象
美国至少有 19 个州,奶牛群和农场工人是受影响人群。
事件
包括德克萨斯大学医学分部的 Gregory Gray 在内的疾病专家正在敦促奶牛群 疫苗接种 防止 H5N1 禽流感 的传播,自 2024 年 3 月以来,该病毒已感染了 1,000 多个奶牛群。
规模、影响与风险
The outbreak has caused a $4 billion hit to the dairy sector and an estimated $14 billion total economic impact, with over 190 million birds killed since 2022. The CDC has reported 71 human cases with two deaths, mostly among farm workers, and the virus poses a risk to humans through potential adaptation in mammals.
应对、状态与不确定性
一些 疫苗 候选药物正在美国农业部进行现场试验,但尚未获得批准。疾病专家强调行动的紧迫性,但 疫苗接种 的有效性和时间表仍在调查中。

Livestock Animal Health Bird flu vaccine for dairy herds urged as virus spreads Farm Progress America: Disease experts say vaccinating cattle could be the most important step to stop H5N1's advance across US farms. April 17, 2026 The bird flu crisis that started in poultry barns three years ago has evolved into a serious dairy problem, and some disease experts now say it's time to start vaccinating cattle. Dr. Gregory Gray from the University of Texas Medical Branch recently argued in the Journal of Infectious Diseases that vaccinating dairy herds may be the single most important step America can take to get ahead of H5N1 avian influenza. The numbers tell a concerning story. Since the virus jumped into dairy cattle in March 2024, more than 1,000 herds across at least 19 states have been infected. The dairy sector alone has taken a $4 billion hit, part of an estimated $14 billion total economic impact from the outbreak that's killed over 190 million birds since 2022. What worries Dr. Gray most is what happens next. Every infection in a mammal gives the virus another chance to adapt. He describes dairy cattle as a potential training ground for H5N1 to learn how to live in mammals, which increases the risk to humans. The CDC has already tallied 71 human cases with two deaths, mostly among farm workers. Related:USDA restores prevented planting buy-up option for crop insurance The good news? Vaccination could work. Studies show infected cows develop strong immunity lasting a year or longer, and early vaccine trials are producing promising results. Several candidates are currently in USDA field trials, though none are approved yet. Dr. Gray's message is clear: the longer we wait, the harder this gets. Since the virus jumped into dairy cattle in March 2024, and the article was published on August 6, 2026.

Event

Scale, impact and risk

The CDC has reported 71 human cases with two deaths, mostly among farm workers, and the virus poses a risk to humans through potential adaptation in mammals.
Response, status and uncertainty
Several vaccine candidates are in USDA field trials, but none are approved yet. Disease experts emphasize the urgency of action, but the effectiveness and timeline of vaccination remain under investigation.

Australia confirms 1st mass mortality of wildlife from bird flu - Anadolu Ajansı

Australia confirms 1st mass mortality of wildlife from bird flu - Anadolu Ajansı
新闻简报Australia has confirmed its first mass mortality event of wildlife linked to the H5 bird flu, with greater crested terns found on Baudin Rocks, an island off South Australia's southeast coast. Dead and sick birds were detected on Friday, 03 August 2026, during aerial surveillance. Samples collected from the site later tested positive for H5 avian influenza, according to the Australian Broadcasting Corporation (ABC). The country has recorded 74 confirmed cases of the virus, with the majority in South Australia. Federal Agriculture Minister Julie Collins stated that the risk to human health remains low, and the national management plan for H5 bird flu has been activated, with an additional $24 million allocated from state and federal governments. The ministers emphasized that wildlife and natural environment are at risk, and that the spread of the virus is expected to continue, leading to more mass mortality events. This event marks the start of a long, difficult journey for wildlife in Australia.
查看新闻五要素
时间
The event was reported on Monday, 03 August 2026. Dead and sick birds were detected on Friday, 03 August 2026, during aerial surveillance on Baudin Rocks.
地点与对象
大规模 死亡率 事件发生在南澳大利亚东南海岸附近的博丹岩岛屿上。受影响的种群包括大凤头燕鸥和迁徙的亚南极海鸟,例如在南澳大利亚、西澳大利亚和新南威尔士州海岸发现的巨型海燕。
事件
澳大利亚确认了第一批与 H5 禽流感有关的野生动物 死亡率。据澳大利亚广播公司 (ABC) 报道,从该地点收集的样本检测出 H5 禽流感 呈阳性。
规模、影响与风险
Australia has recorded 74 confirmed cases of the virus, with the majority in South Australia. The risk to human health is stated as low by Federal Agriculture Minister Julie Collins. The national management plan for H5 bird flu has been activated, with an additional $24 million allocated from state and federal governments.
应对、状态与不确定性
联邦农业部长和联邦环境部长已承认H5禽流感在野生动物和自然环境中的传播。他们启动了国家管理计划,并强调采取生物安全措施的必要性。这种情况被描述为野生动物影响的漫长而艰难的旅程的开始。

Australia confirms 1st mass mortality of wildlife from bird flu - Anadolu Ajansı

News Brief
Australia has confirmed its first mass mortality event of wildlife linked to the H5 bird flu, with greater crested terns found on Baudin Rocks, an island off South Australia's southeast coast. Dead and sick birds were detected on Friday, 03 August 2026, during aerial surveillance. Samples collected from the site later tested positive for H5 avian influenza, according to the Australian Broadcasting Corporation (ABC). The country has recorded 74 confirmed cases of the virus, with the majority in South Australia. Federal Agriculture Minister Julie Collins stated that the risk to human health remains low, and the national management plan for H5 bird flu has been activated, with an additional $24 million allocated from state and federal governments. The ministers emphasized that wildlife and natural environment are at risk, and that the spread of the virus is expected to continue, leading to more mass mortality events. This event marks the start of a long, difficult journey for wildlife in Australia.
Original source text (English)
World Asia - Pacific Australia confirms 1st mass mortality of wildlife from bird flu Federal Agriculture Minister says risk to human health remains low Saadet Gokce 03 August 2026 • Update : 03 August 2026 İSTANBUL Australia has confirmed its first mass wildlife mass mortality linked to the deadly H5 bird flu, with greater crested terns found on Baudin Rocks, an island off South Australia's southeast coast, local media reported Monday. Dead and sick birds were detected on Friday during aerial surveillance on Baudin Rocks, about 5 kilometers (3 miles) south of Cape Jaffa. Samples collected from the site later tested positive for H5 avian influenza, according to the Australian Broadcasting Corporation (ABC). Australia, which remained the only continent free of the deadly virus until June 14, had since only detected bird flu in migratory subantarctic seabirds, mostly giant petrels, found on the coasts of South Australian, Western Australia and New South Wales. The country has recorded 74 confirmed cases of the virus so far, including 54 in South Australia, 10 in Western Australia, seven in Victoria, two in New South Wales, and one in Queensland. Federal Agriculture Minister Julie Collins said the mass mortality was expected. "Sadly, we do know that once the H5 bird flu is spreading in wildlife and the natural environment, it is not possible to avoid significant losses, which is what we're starting to see now." Collins added that there was no evidence of the bird flu in poultry or agricultural systems and the risk to human health remained low. "We have invested and worked really closely with our agriculture, particularly our poultry sector, in terms of improved and increasing biosecurity to do everything we can do to keep it out of our agriculture system,” she said. "But you do tend to see spillover impacts across the globe when it comes to the H5." Collins said Australians could expect to see bird flu continue to spread, with larger numbers of wildlife affected. "The reality is you can't stop native and wild birds flying around, and you can't stop wild animals moving around, and that will mean more mass mortality events like the one we have now seen," Federal Environment Minister Murray Watt also said, adding that this is the beginning of "what is likely to be a long, difficult journey in terms of the impacts on wildlife." He said the national management plan for H5 bird flu had been activated, with an extra $24 million from state and federal governments. View five news elements
Time
The event was reported on Monday, 03 August 2026. Dead and sick birds were detected on Friday, 03 August 2026, during aerial surveillance on Baudin Rocks.
Location and population
The mass mortality event occurred on Baudin Rocks, an island off South Australia's southeast coast. The affected population includes greater crested terns and migratory subantarctic seabirds, such as giant petrels, found on the coasts of South Australia, Western Australia, and New South Wales.
Event
Australia confirmed its first mass mortality of wildlife linked to the H5 bird flu. Samples collected from the site tested positive for H5 avian influenza, as reported by the Australian Broadcasting Corporation (ABC).
Scale, impact and risk
Australia has recorded 74 confirmed cases of the virus, with the majority in South Australia. The risk to human health is stated as low by Federal Agriculture Minister Julie Collins. The national management plan for H5 bird flu has been activated, with an additional $24 million allocated from state and federal governments.
Response, status and uncertainty
The Federal Agriculture Minister and Federal Environment Minister have acknowledged the spread of H5 bird flu in wildlife and the natural environment. They have activated the national management plan and emphasized the need for biosecurity measures. The situation is described as the beginning of a long, difficult journey for wildlife impacts.

澳大利亚致命禽流感病毒株:哪些鸟类通常携带 禽流感 - 印度时报

Deadly bird flu strain in Australia: Which bird species usually carry avian influenza - The Times of India
新闻简报截至2026年8月1日,澳大利亚已确认野生海鸟出现53例高致病性H5禽流感病例。首例检测是西澳大利亚勒格兰德角国家公园一只患病并随后死亡的棕色贼鸥。此后,该病毒已传播到其他地区,包括南澳大利亚、新南威尔士和昆士兰,大凤头燕鸥、海燕和贼鸥均出现确诊病例。联邦科学与工业研究组织 (CSIRO) 澳大利亚疾病预防中心 (ACDP) 的测试发现,南澳大利亚罗布的一只银鸥检测结果呈阳性。值得注意的是,在家禽或农业系统中尚未检测到病毒,对人类健康的风险仍然很低。暴发 是更大的全球 禽流感 事件的一部分,其中已识别出 H5N1 进化枝 2.3.4.4b 毒株。人感染禽流感的情况很少见,人与人之间的传播更是罕见,海外最后一次确诊病例可以追溯到2016年。澳大利亚疾病控制中心强调,自2026年6月以来,尚未报告与这种新毒株有关的人间病例。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026年6月,澳大利亚确认首次在野生海鸟中检测到高致病性H5禽流感,此后该数字不断攀升。截至2026年8月1日,有关部门已确认全国野生海鸟感染H5禽流感病例53例。
地点与对象
澳大利亚,特别是西澳大利亚州、南澳大利亚州、新南威尔士州和昆士兰州的野生海鸟。在家禽或农业生产系统中未检测到。
事件
澳大利亚确认了 53 例野生海鸟感染 H5 禽流感病例,第一例是在西澳大利亚埃斯佩兰斯附近的大角国家公园发现的一只棕色贼鸥,它患病并随后死亡。澳大利亚联邦科学与工业研究组织 (CSIRO) 澳大利亚疾病预防中心 (ACDP) 的检测结果显示,在南澳大利亚罗布发现的一只银鸥呈阳性。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 跨越多个地区和鸟类,在大凤头燕鸥、海燕和贼鸥中都有确诊病例。对人类健康的风险仍然很低,因为自 2026 年 6 月以来,没有出现与这种新毒株有关的人类病例,而且人类感染禽流感的情况也很少见。
应对、状态与不确定性
当局正在监测病毒在野生海鸟中的传播情况,并表示对人类健康的风险仍然很低。尚未报告与这种新菌株有关的人类病例,在家禽或农业系统中也没有检测到这种情况。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Deadly bird flu strain in Australia: Which bird species usually carry avian influenza - The Times of India

News Brief
Australia has confirmed 53 cases of high pathogenicity H5 bird flu in wild seabirds as of August 1, 2026. The first detection was a brown skua found sick and later dead at Cape Le Grand National Park in Western Australia. Since then, the virus has spread to other regions, including South Australia, New South Wales, and Queensland, with confirmed cases in greater crested terns, petrels, and skuas. Testing at CSIRO’s Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness (ACDP) found one positive result in a silver gull at Robe in South Australia. Notably, there have been no detections in poultry or agricultural systems, and the risk to human health remains low. The outbreak is part of a larger global avian influenza event, with H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b being the strain identified. Human infections from bird flu are rare, and person-to-person spread is even rarer, with the last confirmed case overseas dating back to 2016. Australia's Centre for Disease Control has emphasized that no human cases linked to this newer strain have been reported since June 2026.
Original source text (English)

Games Today's ePaper Live Trending Akhilesh Yadav Nagpur Rape Case Abhijeet Dipke Tasmac Liquor Online Purchase JPSC Protest News Health News Deadly bird flu strain in Australia: Which bird species usually carry avian influenza Deadly bird flu strain in Australia: Which bird species usually carry avian influenza TIMESOFINDIA.COM / Aug 01, 2026, 11:42 IST Comments Share AA +Text Size Small Medium Large In June 2026, Australia confirmed its first-ever detections of high pathogenicity H5 bird flu in wild seabirds, and since then the numbers have kept climbing.As of Aug 1, authorities have confirmed 53 cases of H5 bird flu in wild seabirds across the country, according to the Department of Agriculture, Fisheries and Forestry. “Testing at CSIRO’s Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness (ACDP) has returned one positive result in a silver gull found at Robe in South Australia. There have now been 53 confirmed or presumed positive detections of H5 bird flu in Australia. This is the first confirmed detection in a silver gull in Australia. There remain no detections in poultry or in our agricultural production system. The risk to human health remains low,” the DAFF said on Aug 1. How it actually got here Minister for Agriculture, Fisheries and Forestry Julie Collins told a news conference that transmission had occurred between greater crested terns in South Australia state.The first confirmed case was a brown skua found sick, and later dead, at Cape Le Grand National Park near Esperance in Western Australia. Not long after, a giant petrel in the same area tested positive too. From there, the virus has kept showing up in new spots and new birds, with confirmed cases now spanning Western Australia, South Australia, New South Wales, and Queensland. by Taboolaby Taboola Sponsored LinksSponsored Links Promoted LinksPromoted Links You May Like I Added This High‐Risk Biotech To My Watchlist [See my reasoning]VolitionRx Learn More Undo The birds carrying it So which species are actually involved here? Mostly seabirds, and that pattern's held steady since the first case. Terns have been especially hard hit, greater crested terns have turned up positive in multiple locations across South Australia, including Cape Jaffa, Southend, and Port MacDonnell, according to Animal Health Australia. Petrels are another recurring name on the list, with a dead petrel on Moreton Island marking Queensland's first confirmed case. Skuas, like the very first bird that tested positive back in June, are also considered a species of concern.More broadly, wild waterbirds and seabirds are the ones epidemiologists watch most closely with any bird flu strain, because they're the natural hosts for lower pathogenicity versions of the virus and can carry it without showing obvious signs of illness. Migratory birds specifically tend to be the ones that introduce a new strain into a previously unaffected region, simply because of how far and how often they travel. Why this particular strain worries people H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b, the strain now confirmed in Australia, is the same one that's caused mass mortality events in wild birds and even mammals like seals across other continents over the past few years. It's part of what's being called the largest and most widespread avian influenza outbreak ever recorded.Australia's Centre for Disease Control has been clear that human infections from bird flu remain rare, and person-to-person spread is rarer still, with the last confirmed case of that happening overseas dating back to 2016. The country did report one human H5N1 infection back in 2024, though that person had picked it up while travelling abroad and made a full recovery. There haven't been any human cases linked to this newer strain circulating since June.
