View five news elements
Time
In June 2026, Australia confirmed its first-ever detections of high pathogenicity H5 bird flu in wild seabirds, and since then the numbers have kept climbing. As of Aug 1, 2026, authorities have confirmed 53 cases of H5 bird flu in wild seabirds across the country.
Location and population
Australia, specifically wild seabirds across Western Australia, South Australia, New South Wales, and Queensland. No detections in poultry or agricultural production systems.
Event
Australia confirmed 53 cases of H5 bird flu in wild seabirds, with the first case being a brown skua found sick and later dead at Cape Le Grand National Park near Esperance in Western Australia. Testing at CSIRO’s Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness (ACDP) has returned one positive result in a silver gull found at Robe in South Australia.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak spans multiple regions and bird species, with confirmed cases in greater crested terns, petrels, and skuas. The risk to human health remains low, as there have been no human cases linked to this newer strain since June 2026, and human infections from bird flu are rare.
Response, status and uncertainty
Authorities are monitoring the spread of the virus in wild seabirds and have stated that the risk to human health remains low. No human cases linked to this newer strain have been reported, and there are no detections in poultry or agricultural systems.

禽流感的传播:了解密西西比迁飞路线的季节性高致病性禽流感风险 - 俄克拉荷马州农场报告

Bird Flu on the Move: Understanding Seasonal HPAI Risk in the Mississippi Flyway - Oklahoma Farm Report
新闻简报关于密西西比迁飞路线中的 高致病性禽流感 (HPAI) H5N1 进化枝 2.3.4.4b 的季节性风险简报警告生产者注意与水禽迁徙有关的反复出现的 暴发。该病毒于 2022 年首次在美国家禽中检测到,通过 野生鸟类 传播,该病毒在感染后存活并迁移,从 10 月到 5 月扩大了检测范围。虽然夏季检测数量很少,但从秋季到春季的迁徙推动了迁徙路线的更广泛传播,该迁徙路线横跨美国十五个州和加拿大两个省,并支持主要的商业家禽部门。种群减少造成的经济损失导致鸡蛋价格上涨,这凸显了高峰前生物安全升级、财务应急计划和警惕鸡群监测的必要性。该指南由农业经济学家撰写,强调社区做好准备并遵守美国农业部的报告和赔偿计划,以减轻反复出现的季节性风险。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-08-04;该事件被描述为持续的季节性风险,具有 2014 年至 2015 年和 2022 年至今的历史背景。
地点与对象
密西西比飞行路线,覆盖美国十五个以上州和加拿大两个省的部分地区;包括商业肉鸡、火鸡和鸡蛋生产区域。
事件
关于密西西比迁飞路线中 高致病性禽流感 (HPAI) H5N1 进化枝 2.3.4.4b 季节性风险的指导文章,包括历史 暴发 背景和建议的生物安全措施。
规模、影响与风险
鸡蛋价格上涨与人口减少对经济造成影响;HPAI 检测的季节性模式反映了水禽迁徙,夏季检测稀疏,秋季到春季迁徙期间地理分布更广泛;所提供的证据中没有报告病例或死亡人数。
应对、状态与不确定性
建议采取的行动包括旺季前生物安全检查、减少种群数量的财务规划、旺季期间的警惕监测以及全年参与美国农业部 APHIS 检测报告;承认由于 宿主 适应病毒在迁徙 野生鸟类 中反复出现季节性风险的不确定性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird Flu on the Move: Understanding Seasonal HPAI Risk in the Mississippi Flyway - Oklahoma Farm Report

News Brief
A seasonal risk briefing on highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b in the Mississippi Flyway warns producers of recurring outbreaks tied to waterfowl migration. The virus, first detected in U.S. poultry in 2022, spreads via wild birds that survive infection and migrate, expanding geographic detections from October through May. While summer detections are sparse, fall-to-spring migration drives broader spread across the flyway, which spans over fifteen U.S. states and two Canadian provinces and supports major commercial poultry sectors. Economic losses from depopulation contribute to elevated egg prices, underscoring the need for pre-peak biosecurity upgrades, financial contingency planning, and vigilant flock monitoring. Authored by agricultural economists, the guidance emphasizes community preparedness and adherence to USDA reporting and indemnity programs to mitigate recurring seasonal risk.
Original source text (English)
Toggle search form Search for: Toggle navigation Oklahoma Farm Report Home Listen to Us Markets Ag News Ag News Agri-Innovations Blue Green Gazette Weather Calendar Auctions Daily Emails Daily Emails Archives Subscribe to Daily Email About Us About Us Our Team Oklahoma Farm Report Radio Affiliates Beef Buzz Contact Contact Us Advertise With Us Email Us a Story/Idea/Content Congressman Brecheen August Town Halls – Oklahoma – 8/24-26, 2026 National FFA Reaches Record 1.05 Million Student Members Across Nearly 9,600 Chapters Aug 04 Bird Flu on the Move: Understanding Seasonal HPAI Risk in the Mississippi Flyway Jada Thompson, Associate Professor, Department of Agricultural Economics and Agribusiness Ryan Loy, Assistant Professor and Extension Economist, Department of Agricultural Economics and Agribusiness Highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI), commonly called bird flu refers to the H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b strain of avian influenza, which was detected in the United States in 2022. The term “highly pathogenic” refers to the virus’s ability to spread quickly and destructively, killing more than 75% of birds it infects (World Organisation for Animal Health, 2026). Because the virus is so deadly in commercial poultry, best practice requires the depopulation of flocks in which the disease is detected. Primarily, this is the humane response, as the entire flock will perish if given enough time. Depopulation also stops the reproduction of the virus, protecting farmers, consumers, and the public. Additionally, it allows farmers to minimize down time, returning farms to production as soon as possible, after appropriate cleaning and disinfection. Though necessary, these population losses lead to economic consequences which extend well beyond just individual farms, contributing significantly to elevated egg prices (Mitchell et al., 2024). Bird Flu in the Flyway The U.S. poultry industry encountered H5N2 HPAI in 2014, which was mostly spread house-to-house through contaminated materials, trucks, and laborers. This prompted sweeping changes in biosecurity culture that still exist today. These changes, along with a single warm summer, helped to end the outbreak by June 2015. Since the 2022 introduction of a host-adapted H5N1 virus, poultry growers in the Mississippi Flyway have faced increased disease risk, despite the wide adoption of these enhanced biosecurity measures. This most recent outbreak has spread instead by external introduction, rather than in the same fashion as in 2014. In this case, “host-adapted” refers to the virus’s ability to persist in wild bird populations. Whereas previous HPAI events ravaged both poultry and wild birds, the current H5N1 2.3.4.4b classification is less likely to kill wild bird species (Patyk et al., 2023; Thompson et al., 2025). This means that waterfowl can become infected and still survive migration, spreading the disease across the flyway. What is the Mississippi flyway? Discover more Farm Farms & Ranches Agriculture & Forestry Migratory birds follow predictable migratory corridors, called flyways. The Mississippi flyway is an economically important flyway, covering parts of more than fifteen states and two Canadian provinces which include commercial broiler, turkey, and egg production. Migratory fowl spend the warm summer months in the breeding grounds of the northern Midwest, leaving in the fall to ride cold fronts south towards over-wintering habitat in the Gulf States. For southern states like Arkansas and Louisiana, the regularity of this migration is a large source of off-farm income, as waterfowl hunters flock to the Mississippi Delta. The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service estimates that migratory bird hunters in Arkansas spend nearly $300 million annually on lodging, equipment, food, and transportation, over the state’s 72-day waterfowl season (U.S. Fish & Wildlife Service, 2022). Birds begin moving north again in the spring, often returning to the same breeding region year after year. Aside from its incredible biodiversity during migrations, the wetland habitat along the Mississippi River acts as a natural flood barrier year-round, holding surplus water as it drains towards the river. Examining the geographic distribution of HPAI detections over time reveals a clear seasonal pattern that mirrors waterfowl migration. Figure 1 shows the number of detections, by county, for each month over the course of the outbreak. During the summer months, detections are sparse and concentrated in the northern reaches of the flyway, where birds spend the breeding season. As fall migration begins, HPAI’s geographic footprint expands southward, spanning nearly the entire flyway from October through May. Figure 1: Mississippi Flyway HPAI Detections by Month, 2022-2025, with fitted latitudinal mean and IQR What does this mean? Understanding seasonality affords producers a predictable window each year to prepare before peak exposure periods arrive. Before Peak Season Inspect and repair perimeter fencing, netting, and housing gaps that could allow wildlife to access farm premises. Evaluate water and feed delivery systems to ensure they too are isolated from outside contact. Review all available extension publications, updating biosecurity protocols where necessary while maintaining coverage status with livestock indemnity programs. Conduct training and review these protocols with all farm workers and establish relationships with your veterinarian and state animal health officials before an emergency arises. Budget for increased biosecurity expenditures and most importantly, ensure you have a financial emergency plan, should you face depopulation. During Peak Season Vigilantly adhere to enhanced biosecurity protocols without exception. Monitor flocks closely for behavioral changes, decreased feed and water consumption, or sudden mortality. Limit all non-essential personnel traffic and enforce strict entry and exit biosecurity for all visitors and vehicles. Report any unusual mortality to your veterinarian or the USDA at 1-866-536-7593. Year-Round Stay current with USDA APHIS detection reports to understand active disease pressure in your region. Participate in state and federal livestock indemnity programs and know your coverage. The Big Picture A host-adapted virus in a migratory population means recurring seasonal risk for the foreseeable future. However, that seasonality also gives producers, researchers, and communities time to plan ahead. Understanding when and where the disease is likely to appear turns an unpredictable force into a manageable calendar of preparation and vigilance. Extension agents and agricultural educators can use these materials to help producers understand local risk windows and connect with available resources. Community preparedness, which begins at the farm level, remains the most effective tool available against this disease. Location and population
Mississippi Flyway, covering parts of more than fifteen U.S. states and two Canadian provinces; includes commercial broiler, turkey, and egg production regions.
Event
Guidance article on seasonal risk of highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b in the Mississippi Flyway, including historical outbreak context and recommended biosecurity measures.
Scale, impact and risk
Economic impact noted via elevated egg prices linked to depopulation; seasonal pattern of HPAI detections mirrors waterfowl migration, with sparse summer detections and broader geographic spread during fall-to-spring migration; no case or death counts reported in the supplied evidence.
Response, status and uncertainty

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

禽流感导致南美洲的海洋哺乳动物大量死亡。澳大利亚可以采取什么不同的做法? - 对话

Bird flu decimated South America’s marine mammals. What can Australia do differently? - The Conversation
新闻简报The Conversation 于 2026 年 8 月 4 日发表的一篇新闻报道讨论了 H5N1 禽流感在南美洲的传播及其对澳大利亚海洋哺乳动物的潜在影响。文章强调,H5N1 病毒已导致鳍足类动物大规模死亡,其中包括阿根廷的南象海豹,有超过 17,000 只幼崽死亡。在南美洲,该病毒已导致多个国家至少 30,000 只海狮死亡。该报告警告说,H5N1 病毒可能会传播到澳大利亚的鳍足类动物,而这些动物已经受到其他环境因素的威胁。澳大利亚当局正在密切关注事态发展,南澳大利亚东南海岸首次确认大凤头燕鸥大规模死亡。文章强调需要采取生物安全措施,以防止病毒到达澳大利亚海洋哺乳动物群落,这些哺乳动物群落因其生态和保护状况而脆弱。它还指出,虽然人类感染的风险仍然很低,但该病毒适应哺乳动物 宿主 的能力是潜在更广泛的 暴发 的担忧。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布时间:美国东部时间 2026 年 8 月 4 日下午 4:05 •更新日期:美国东部时间 2026 年 8 月 4 日晚上 10:12。
地点与对象
南美洲、澳大利亚,特别是袋鼠岛和维多利亚州的菲利普港湾和吉普斯兰地区。
事件
澳大利亚大陆的 野生鸟类 发现了 H5N1 禽流感,这是南澳大利亚东南海岸首例大凤头燕鸥大规模死亡事件。该病毒还导致南美鳍足类动物大规模死亡,其中包括阿根廷的南象海豹。
规模、影响与风险
在暴发期间,秘鲁、智利、阿根廷、乌拉圭和巴西至少有30,000只南美海狮死亡。在阿根廷,超过17,000只南象海豹幼崽死亡。该病毒已在感染的鳍足类动物中引发严重的呼吸系统疾病和神经系统症状,并在尸检中观察到明显的肺部病变。人类感染的风险仍然较低,但监测工作至关重要,以防止更大规模的大流行。
应对、状态与不确定性
当局正在对维多利亚州西南部患病和死亡的燕鸥进行进一步检测。建议采取生物安全措施来保护鳍足类动物,包括监测、清除尸体以及处理野生动物的严格协议。出于生物安全考虑,没有对阿根廷海豹进行全面尸检,因此死因不能明确归因于病毒。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird flu decimated South America’s marine mammals. What can Australia do differently? - The Conversation

News Brief
A news report from The Conversation, published on August 4, 2026, discusses the spread of H5N1 bird flu in South America and its potential impact on Australia’s marine mammals. The article highlights that H5N1 has caused mass deaths among pinnipeds, including southern elephant seals in Argentina, with over 17,000 pups dying. In South America, the virus has led to the death of at least 30,000 sea lions in several countries. The report warns that H5N1 could spread to Australia’s pinnipeds, which are already under threat from other environmental factors. Authorities in Australia are monitoring the situation, with the first confirmed mass death of greater crested terns reported off the south-east coast of South Australia. The article emphasizes the need for biosecurity measures to prevent the virus from reaching Australia’s marine mammal colonies, which are vulnerable due to their ecological and conservation status. It also notes that while the risk of human infection remains low, the virus’s ability to adapt to mammalian hosts is a concern for potential broader outbreaks.
Original source text (English)

Home Arts + Culture Economy Education Environment + Energy Ethics + Religion Health Politics + Society Science + Tech World Podcasts Local En Español Edition Africa Australia Brasil Canada Canada (français) Català España Europe France Global Indonesia New Zealand United Kingdom United States Donate Skip to content Edition: United States Africa Australia Brasil Canada Canada (français) Català España Europe France Global Indonesia New Zealand United Kingdom s Donate Newsletters Become an author Sign up as a reader Sign in Academic rigor, journalistic flair Arts + Culture Economy Education Environment + Energy Ethics + Religion Health Politics + Society Science + Tech World Podcasts Local Foto4440/Getty Bird flu decimated South America’s marine mammals. What can Australia do differently? Published: August 4, 2026 4:05pm EDT •Updated: August 4, 2026 10:12pm EDT Australia is now confronting an outbreak of a deadly bird flu strain. As of Monday, the highly pathogenic H5N1 virus had been detected in 78 wild birds across mainland Australia. Until now, there had been no evidence of a large-scale outbreak. But authorities have confirmed the first instance of mass death caused by H5N1 among dozens of greater crested terns off South Australia’s south-east coast. They are also conducting further testing on a cluster of sick and dead terns in south-west Victoria. As H5N1 spreads across the country, researchers, wildlife managers and the broader public are increasingly concerned about the risks it may pose beyond birds. Recent outbreaks have threatened aquatic mammals, including southern otters, dolphins and porpoises. H5N1 has also decimated pinniped populations around the globe, including in South America. Pinnipeds are marine mammals that live in the sea and form colonies but spend extended periods on land, such as sea lions, fur seals and elephant seals. As the risk of a broader outbreak rises, Australia must protect its wildlife by drawing on lessons from other continents already battling bird flu. Looking to South America H5N1 poses an urgent threat to wildlife worldwide. Since 2021, this fatal strain of bird flu has spread to every continent and infected poultry flocks, dairy cattle, wild birds and a growing range of wild mammals. In June, it finally reached Australia, first detected in a sick brown skua. This strain has been particularly devastating in South America. In late 2022, H5N1 spread rapidly from infected seabirds into pinniped colonies. At least 30,000 South American sea lions died in Peru, Chile, Argentina, Uruguay and Brazil during the subsequent outbreak. In some areas, major breeding colonies lost up to one third of their population within a single breeding season. Two sea lions from a South American sea lion breeding colony. Martín Brogger Current evidence suggests H5N1 also caused mass deaths in southern elephant seals – the same species as Neil the seal – with more than 17,000 pups dying at Península Valdés in Argentina. Scientists still don’t know how many adults died. Our team of researchers – from the Universidad Nacional del Comahue in Argentina and Deakin University in Australia – documented a dramatic decline in the number of seals in northern Patagonia following the 2022-23 outbreak. Subsequent studies across South America show infected pinnipeds developed severe neurological and respiratory disease. Symptoms include lethargy, tremors, impaired coordination and laboured breathing, among others. Affected animals showed severe respiratory disease and neurological signs, and post-mortem examinations revealed marked lung lesions. Many animals died within a short period after clinical signs appeared. Read more: The warning signs are over: Australia now faces a devastating bird flu outbreak in wildlife Australian mammals at risk Though confronting, the South American outbreak gives us crucial insight into how H5N1 spreads, which species may be most vulnerable and what strategies could reduce its impacts. Each time H5N1 infects and replicates in a mammal, the virus becomes better at adapting to mammalian hosts. Monitoring how efficiently it spreads between mammals is therefore essential for protecting wildlife. It also allows us to assess the risk of human infection — which currently remains low — and the potential for a broader pandemic. Two Australian sea lions (Neophoca cinerea) resting on a beach. Henrik Karlsson/Getty For now, H5N1 has not yet reached Australia’s pinniped colonies, which are already under pressure from fishery nets and hooks, pollution, habitat destruction and climate change. So we still have time to protect them from H5N1. Authorities should start with Kangaroo Island, where infected seabirds have been found near the breeding habitat of the endangered Australian sea lion. Other threatened marine mammals may also be vulnerable. This includes the rare Burrunan dolphin, of which there are less than 200 individual animals swimming around Victoria’s Port Phillip Bay and Gippsland regions. There are fewer than 200 Burrunan dolphins left in the wild. tracielouise/Getty So, how can we protect them? As Australia’s number of confirmed H5N1 cases rises, urgent action is needed to protect our vulnerable marine mammals. Australian authorities can learn from the South American outbreak by: frequently conducting population censuses, to detect unusual deaths and sudden changes in the size of seal and sea lion colonies closely monitoring live animals and taking samples of any dead animals, to track how the virus is circulating before any mass deaths happen removing all carcasses, particularly near tourist areas, to stop scavenging birds, dogs and animals from being exposed to infected animals ensuring researchers, wildlife managers and tourism operators stick to strict biosecurity protocols, such as wearing appropriate personal protective equipment when handling sick or dead wildlife and disinfecting boots and equipment between sites tightening biosecurity measures around food production, particularly intensive poultry farms where commercial poultry may be exposed to infected wild birds. The public can also help protect our vulnerable pinnipeds by: not approaching any sick wildlife keeping pets away from wildlife reporting any sightings of sick or dead birds, seals, sea lions or other animals to the 24-hour Emergency Animal Disease Hotline by ringing 1800 675 888, noting details about the date, location, type and number of animals. As the number of infected seabirds rises, so does the threat of a H5N1 outbreak among Australia’s marine mammal colonies. South America reveals how devastating this virus can be for entire ecosystems. Australia should heed that warning while there is still time to protect our precious pinnipeds. Read more: Scavenger animals are the missing link in Australia’s bird flu response. Three experts explain Correction: This article originally stated post-mortem examinations of infected pinnipeds revealed the virus directly affected animals’ brains and lungs, and could lead to death within a short period. This has been corrected to say post-mortem examinations of infected pinnipeds revealed marked lung lesions, and many of them died within a short period after clinical signs appeared. Full necropsies were not carried out on the Argentinian seals due to biosecurity concerns, meaning the cause of death cannot be definitively attributed to the virus. Time
Published: August 4, 2026 4:05pm EDT •Updated: August 4, 2026 10:12pm EDT.
Location and population
South America, Australia, particularly Kangaroo Island and Victoria’s Port Phillip Bay and Gippsland regions.
Event
H5N1 bird flu has been detected in wild birds across mainland Australia, with the first instance of mass death among greater crested terns off South Australia’s south-east coast. The virus has also caused mass deaths in pinnipeds in South America, including southern elephant seals in Argentina.
Scale, impact and risk
At least 30,000 South American sea lions died in Peru, Chile, Argentina, Uruguay and Brazil during the outbreak. In Argentina, more than 17,000 pups of southern elephant seals died. The virus has caused severe respiratory disease and neurological signs in infected pinnipeds, with marked lung lesions observed in post-mortem examinations. The risk of human infection remains low, but monitoring is essential to prevent broader pandemics.
Response, status and uncertainty
Authorities are conducting further testing on sick and dead terns in south-west Victoria. Biosecurity measures are being recommended to protect pinnipeds, including monitoring, removing carcasses, and strict protocols for handling wildlife. Full necropsies on Argentinian seals were not carried out due to biosecurity concerns, so the cause of death cannot be definitively attributed to the virus.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

密歇根州农民面临预防和遏制禽流感等疾病的新规则 - WWMT

Michigan farmers facing new rules to prevent and contain disease outbreaks like bird flu - WWMT
新闻简报密歇根州农业和农村发展部 (MDARD) 正在审查公认农业和管理实践 (GAAMP) 和公认加工实践 (GAPP) 的更新。这些拟议的变更旨在将 2024 年 禽流感 暴发 期间使用的紧急生物安全指令纳入永久州农业指南。这些更新表明,商业乳制品和家禽经营机构实施并记录了日常安全协议和应急响应策略,以预防和遏制疾病 暴发。建议的措施包括维护围栏等物理屏障,并实施强化的安全协议,例如更换衣服和靴子,以降低传播风险。尽管 MDARD 指出,这些更新的指南是自愿建议,旨在推广最佳实践和积极的农业形象,但它们旨在帮助该行业为已知病毒和未来新出现的疾病做好准备。该州目前正在审查 2027 年的这些草案更新,并邀请公众向 MDARD 农场权利计划提交书面意见。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 31 日;更新于 2026 年 8 月 1 日。参考 2024 年 禽流感 暴发 和 2027 年 GAAMP 草案。
地点与对象
密歇根州,特别是商业乳制品和家禽经营。
事件
密歇根州农业和农村发展部 (MDARD) 正在审查公认农业和管理实践 (GAAMP) 和公认加工实践 (GAPP) 的年度编辑。这包括拟议的更新,将 2024 年 禽流感 暴发 中的紧急生物安全指令纳入永久州农业指南。
规模、影响与风险
这些措施旨在预防和遏制禽流感等疾病。2024 年 暴发 要求商业乳制品和家禽经营立即提高生物安全。拟议的指南建议农民正式制定日常和紧急生物安全协议,例如使用栅栏、障碍物和更换衣服/靴子来防止疾病传播。
应对、状态与不确定性
MDARD 目前正在审查 2027 年 GAAMP 的更新草案。拟议的措施是自愿建议,旨在推广最佳实践并为已知和未知疾病做好准备。公众意见可以提交给 MDARD 农场权利计划。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Michigan farmers facing new rules to prevent and contain disease outbreaks like bird flu - WWMT

News Brief
The Michigan Department of Agriculture and Rural Development (MDARD) is reviewing updates to the Generally Accepted Agricultural and Management Practices (GAAMPs) and Generally Accepted Processing Practices (GAPPs). These proposed changes aim to integrate emergency biosecurity mandates used during the 2024 avian influenza outbreak into permanent state agricultural guidelines. The updates suggest that commercial dairy and poultry operations implement and document both daily safety protocols and emergency response strategies to prevent and contain disease outbreaks. Recommended measures include maintaining physical barriers like fences and implementing heightened security protocols, such as changing clothing and boots, to mitigate the risk of spread. While MDARD noted that these updated guidelines are voluntary suggestions intended to promote best practices and a positive agricultural image, they are designed to prepare the industry for both known viruses and future emerging diseases. The state is currently reviewing these draft updates for 2027, and the public is invited to submit written comments to the MDARD Right to Farm Program.
Original source text (English)

surprised when the virus, which is common in wild birds, was discovered in dairy cattle, poultry, and even people. (WWMT) 2Comment Share topics: Michigan Birdflu Biosecurity GAAMPs MDARD Emergencyorders Farmers Publiccomment LANSING, Mich. — The emergency biosecurity mandates that swept Michigan farms during the 2024 avian influenza outbreak could soon become permanently integrated into state agricultural guidelines. The Michigan Department of Agriculture and Rural Development (MDARD) is currently reviewing its annual edits to the Generally Accepted Agricultural and Management Practices, commonly known as GAAMPs, and Generally Accepted Processing Practices (GAPPs). This year's draft includes a variety of best-practice updates, including recommending farmers implement and document biosecurity measures, to prevent the spread of diseases. According to an officials from MDARD and MSU Extension, it is ideal for commercial farms of any size to have both a daily safety protocol and to establish reliable emergency response strategies, in case of abnormal outbreaks. Draft 2027 GAAMPs summary of proposed changesPDF previewPreview PDF View PDF Previous Coverage: Michigan farms help USDA in research of bird flu outbreak spreading to cattle and humans State-issued emergency orders in 2024 required commercial dairy and poultry operations to immediately increase biosecurity to combat bird flu. Many farmers were left scrambling to implement new protocol. Agricultural officials say emergency actions are necessary to contain the spread of disease, but daily biosecurity measures play an important role in prevention. Among the recommendations drafted in this year's GAAMPs, they are suggesting farmers formalize, on paper, their own approach to daily and emergency biosecurity. Between COVID-19 and bird flu to recent cyclophorias cases, many have come to realize the importance of preventing and responding to widespread disease outbreaks. That's something MDARD says the state is focused on, too. While MDARD does have authority to address emergency situations, however, the GAAMPs or GAPPs are intended to serve a more general purpose, and are not specific to any outbreak or disease. Moreover, they are simply suggestions. MDARD'S GAAMPs and GAPPs coordinator, Kyle McCarty, points out that these updates are aimed at promoting best practices and prevent public nuisance, as part of an effort to promote a positive image of agriculture in Michigan. An MSU Extension spokesperson said these measures prepare the state, not just for known viruses, but for diseases the industry has yet to encounter. Because modern food sources are highly interconnected, tracking and containment are crucial to pinpointing the origin of an outbreak before it spreads. The updated guidelines encourage farmers to maintain baseline daily biosecurity protocols, including fences and barriers, alongside a heightened security plan that can be enacted immediately if an outbreak occurs, such as changing boots and clothing before and after leaving secure areas of their farms and restricting visitors. "They're voluntary practices that operations of any size could be implementing," McCarty said. More news in West Michigan: What a psychologist wants you to know about the Grand Haven family murder-suicide Because writing a biosecurity plan from scratch can be overwhelming, the GAAMPs committee drafted 'Appendix A' to serve as a template that farm operators can use to start building their own plan, officials said. It is divided into key action areas: Designation of a Biosecurity Manager: The plan requires the farm to explicitly name the individual (often the owner or herd manager) who is responsible for developing, implementing, and enforcing the biosecurity protocols. Line of Separation (LOS): This requires defining a clear physical or imaginary line that separates the "clean" areas of the farm (where the animals are) from the "dirty" areas (outside world). It asks how the farm plans to enforce this (e.g., gates, signage, locked doors) and how workers transition across this line. Personnel and Visitor Protocols: Sign-in logs for keeping track of who enters the facility; disposable boot covers, footbaths, or dedicated clothing for farm; Downtime requirements for workers who have been exposed to other farm animals. Animal Movement and Isolation: Protocols for quarantining new animals for a set period (usually 14-30 days) before introducing them to the group. Dead Stock Management: Procedures for disposing of deceased animals in a way that prevents scavengers and disease spread. Equipment and Vehicles: Guidelines for cleaning and disinfecting shared equipment before use, especially if they are borrowed from a neighboring farm. Wildlife, Rodent, and Pest Control: Securing feed bins from wild birds, cleaning spills, and managing standing water. Beyond biosecurity on farms, the proposed GAAMPs draft proposes several other updates, such as new spacing between honeybee colonies and recommendations for greenhouses, to prevent potential complaints around noise or light pollution. State officials are actively seeking feedback on the new recommendations to determine how they will affect farmers. Residents and farmers are encouraged to review the draft and provide input before the public comment period closes at the end of August. The announcement from MDARD said public input can be submitted in multiple ways, including: Email: MDARD-RTF@michigan.gov Website: Submit comments on the GAAMPs and/or on the GAPPs U.S. Mail: Send written comments to the MDARD Right to Farm Program, P.O. Box 30017, Lansing, MI 48909 Public Meeting: A virtual public meeting is scheduled for August 27, 2026, at 1 p.m. ET | Link to join the virtual meeting READ THE COMMENTS (2) MDARD will forward all public input received by August 30 deadline, and the respective advisory committees will share their recommendations at the regularly scheduled Michigan Commission of Agriculture and Rural Development meeting on September 9, 2026. Time
Published July 31, 2026; updated August 1, 2026. Refers to 2024 avian influenza outbreak and 2027 draft GAAMPs.
Location and population
Michigan, specifically commercial dairy and poultry operations.
Event
The Michigan Department of Agriculture and Rural Development (MDARD) is reviewing annual edits to the Generally Accepted Agricultural and Management Practices (GAAMPs) and Generally Accepted Processing Practices (GAPPs). This includes proposed updates to integrate emergency biosecurity mandates from the 2024 avian influenza outbreak into permanent state agricultural guidelines.
Scale, impact and risk
The measures aim to prevent and contain disease outbreaks like bird flu. The 2024 outbreak required commercial dairy and poultry operations to immediately increase biosecurity. The proposed guidelines suggest farmers formalize daily and emergency biosecurity protocols, such as using fences, barriers, and changing clothing/boots to prevent disease spread.
Response, status and uncertainty
MDARD is currently reviewing draft updates for the 2027 GAAMPs. The proposed measures are voluntary suggestions intended to promote best practices and prepare for both known and unknown diseases. Public comments can be submitted to the MDARD Right to Farm Program.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Australia: First Mass Mortality Event in Seabirds Due to H5N1 HAPI - PoultryMed

News Brief
Australia has reported its first mass mortality event in seabirds linked to H5N1 HAPI, according to PoultryMed. The event was noted on 03/08/2026, and it highlights the potential risk of this highly pathogenic avian influenza subtype to wildlife. While the exact number of affected seabirds is not specified, the report underscores the importance of monitoring such outbreaks. The source, PoultryMed, is a reputable platform for poultry disease information, but it is a syndicated summary, meaning the full original article could not be accessed. This event is part of a broader context of avian influenza concerns, including other outbreaks and recalls related to poultry diseases. The report does not provide details on specific response measures or containment strategies, indicating that further investigation is needed.

Armageddon has arrived: Australia faces the grim reality of H5 bird flu - afr.com
新闻简报澳大利亚正面临不断升级的 H5N1 禽流感 暴发 病毒,专家警告称,该病毒将不可避免地在 野生鸟类 种群中造成大规模 死亡率,该病毒被描述为势不可挡,可能会影响所有鸟类。《金融评论》报道称,随着 H5N1 病毒在全国范围内蔓延,野生动物专家、科学家和家禽养殖者正在准备应对本土物种和农业部门遭受的毁灭性损失。虽然文章强调了严峻的前景,但提供的证据中没有引用官方病例数、遏制措施或政府应对措施。

澳大利亚(全国范围);野生动物、家禽业和人类社区。
事件
媒体报道描述了 H5N1 禽流感 在澳大利亚的传播,专家警告 野生鸟类 中不可避免的大规模 死亡率 以及对家禽业的潜在严重影响。
规模、影响与风险
野生鸟类的质量死亡率被描述为不可避免;所提供的证据中没有提供具体病例或死亡人数。风险被定义为不受控制的蔓延,没有物种能够幸免。

提供的证据中没有详细说明官方应对行动、测试方案或遏制措施。该报告提出了专家警告,但没有确认干预措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Armageddon has arrived: Australia faces the grim reality of H5 bird flu - afr.com

News Brief
Australia is confronting an escalating H5N1 avian influenza outbreak that experts warn will cause inevitable mass mortality across wild bird populations, with the virus described as unstoppable and likely to affect every bird species. The Financial Review reports that wildlife experts, scientists, and poultry farmers are bracing for devastating losses to native species and the agricultural sector as the H5N1 virus advances nationwide. While the article underscores the grim outlook, no official case counts, containment measures, or government responses are cited in the evidence provided.
